Since April 2023, there has been a [civil war](7085.md) in [Sudan](27421.md) between the two main factions of the country's [military government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_government). The conflict involves the internationally recognized [government of Sudan](5698926.md) led by the [Sudanese Armed Forces](27429.md) (SAF), under General [Abdel Fattah al-Burhan](60480545.md); against the rival [Government of Peace and Unity](79260861.md) led by the [Rapid Support Forces](43053874.md) (RSF) paramilitary group, commanded by General [Hemedti](60669608.md) (Muhammad Hamdan Dagalo Musa). Smaller armed groups have also taken part on both sides, most notably [Sudan Liberation Movement](2031543.md) splinter groups, the [Tamazuj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamazuj) militia, and the [Darfur Joint Protection Force](74949825.md).[^cite_note-45]

Fighting began on 15 April 2023 following a power struggle within the transitional administration established after the [2021 coup](69110231.md). As of 6 July 2026, the conflict has [forcibly displaced](477535.md) over 14 million people, with 4.5 million fleeing the country as refugees,[^cite_note-46] making it one of the largest displacement crises in recent history.[^cite_note-UN-2025-47][^cite_note-48] The conflict has also seen heavy influence from foreign powers, especially [Russia](25391.md),[^cite_note-MEERussia-49] [Turkey](11125639.md),[^cite_note-FDDTurkey-50] [Saudi Arabia](349303.md),[^cite_note-saudiuaesudan-51] [Iran](14653.md),[^cite_note-iran_drones-52] [Egypt](8087628.md),[^cite_note-AJreturn-53] [Ethiopia](187749.md)[^cite_note-Julien-Thomas-2026-54] and the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md),[^cite_note-SkynewsUAE-55] while also experiencing effects from the spillover of neighboring conflicts in the [Central African Republic](5478.md) (CAR),[^cite_note-NYTCAR-56] [Chad](5488.md)[^cite_note-Al-Geneina-57] and [Libya](17633.md).[^cite_note-NABDLibya-58]

The current war erupted amid tensions regarding the integration of the RSF into the Sudanese Army following the 2021 coup. Initial RSF attacks targeted government sites in the capital, [Khartoum](17320.md), and other cities. The conflict began with the [Battle of Khartoum](73563331.md), and fighting then spread to the [Darfur](605837.md) region.[^cite_note-59][^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61] The capital region was divided between the two factions, and al-Burhan relocated his government to [Port Sudan](213572.md). International efforts, including the 2023 Jeddah Declaration, failed to stop the fighting, while various rebel groups entered the war: the [SPLM–North](30884032.md) attacked the SAF in the south; the [Tamazuj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamazuj) movement joined the RSF; and the SAF gained support from factions of the [Sudan Liberation Movement](2031543.md) and [Justice and Equality Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_and_Equality_Movement).

By late 2023, the RSF controlled most of Darfur and advanced on Khartoum, taking over most of the capital, [Kordofan](1127161.md) and [Gezira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gezira_State). The SAF regained momentum in 2024, making gains in [Omdurman](100414.md) and retaking Khartoum by March 2025. Despite negotiations, no lasting ceasefire has been reached, and the war continues with severe humanitarian consequences and regional implications.&#x20;

The city of [El Fasher](3956787.md), the SAF's last stronghold in Darfur, was besieged by the RSF from April 2024. On 26 October 2025, the city fell to the RSF, with the city and neighboring villages suffering what some experts called a [genocidal massacre](81461601.md) inflicting between 60,000 and 70,000 deaths by one estimate.[^cite_note-62][^cite_note-63] Satellite estimates have been as high as 150,000 dead, making it the largest massacre of the 21st century.[^cite_note-64]

Sudan faces one of the world's most severe [humanitarian crises](1251700.md), with widespread [famine](77186018.md) and 25 million people suffering severe food insecurity.[^cite_note-SF-65] Four million children are acutely malnourished, including 770,000 at imminent risk of death, and famine has been confirmed in several regions.[^cite_note-sudchi-42] The country faces extreme shortages of water, medicine and aid access, widespread hospital closures, disease outbreaks, [mass displacement](45547.md), looting of humanitarian supplies, and the near-collapse of education and infrastructure, leaving half the population in urgent need. The total death toll of the war comprises fatalities from [violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violence), [starvation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starvation) and [disease](8072.md); thousands remain missing or were killed in targeted massacres primarily attributed to the RSF and allied militias.[^cite_note-HK-66] 61,000 people have died in [Khartoum State](767612.md) alone, of whom 26,000 were direct victims of violence.[^cite_note-ap20-67] Sexual violence has been widespread.[^cite_note-Sudan-2025b-68][^cite_note-69][^cite_note-70] UN under-secretary for humanitarian affairs Tom Fletcher described Darfur as the "epicenter of human suffering in the world".[^cite_note-Julien-Thomas-2026-54]

There have been calls for more aid, legal protections for [humanitarian workers](434679.md), refugee support and a halt to international arms supplies to the RSF, particularly from the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) (UAE).[^cite_note-Sudan-2025a-71][^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73][^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75] The US, UK, EU and Canada imposed [sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctions_\(law\)) on entities linked to both factions for ceasefire violations and human rights abuses. Despite denials, the UAE has been found to violate these sanctions by shipping Chinese weaponry to the RSF,[^cite_note-Sudan-2025a-71] which largely funds its operations through [gold](12240.md) exports to the UAE.[^cite_note-Julien-Thomas-2026-54] Many civilians in Darfur have been killed as part of the [Masalit genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masalit_genocide).[^cite_note-76] On 7 January 2025, the United States government formally determined that the RSF and its allied militias committed acts of genocide.[^cite_note-77]

## Background

Main articles: [History of Sudan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Sudan) and [Coups d'état in Sudan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coups_d'%C3%A9tat_in_Sudan)

Since gaining independence in 1956, Sudan has endured chronic instability marked by [20 coup attempts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coups_d'%C3%A9tat_in_Sudan) and prolonged military rule, usually interspersed with [short periods of democratic parliamentary rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Sudan).[^cite_note-78][^cite_note-79]

Two civil wars, [1955–1972](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Sudanese_Civil_War) and [1983–2005](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Sudanese_Civil_War), were fought between the [central government](5698926.md) and the [southern regions](32350676.md), killing 1.5 million people and ultimately leading to the independence of South Sudan in 2011. Separately, the [conflict in the western region of Darfur](5043637.md) displaced two million people and killed more than 200,000.[^cite_note-80]

### War in Darfur and the formation of the RSF

By the turn of the 21st century, Sudan's western [Darfur](605837.md) region had endured prolonged instability and social strife due to ethnic tensions and disputes over land and water. In 2003, this situation erupted into a full-scale rebellion, which president and [military strongman](146728.md) [Omar al-Bashir](393625.md) vowed to crush. The resulting [War in Darfur](5043637.md) was marked by widespread state-sponsored violence, leading to charges of [war crimes](48626.md) and [genocide](55472698.md) against al-Bashir.[^cite_note-Abdelaziz-2023-81] The initial phase of the conflict left approximately 300,000 dead and 2.7 million forcibly displaced; even though the intensity of the violence later declined, the situation in the region remained far from peaceful.[^cite_note-PathtoPower-82]

To crush uprisings by non-Arab tribes in the [Nuba Mountains](1139195.md), al-Bashir relied upon the [Janjaweed](683270.md), a collection of [Baggara Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baggara_Arabs) militias drawn from camel-trading tribes active in Darfur and portions of [Chad](5488.md). In 2013, al-Bashir announced that the Janjaweed would be reorganised as the [Rapid Support Forces](43053874.md) (RSF) under the command of the Janjaweed's commander General [Hemedti](60669608.md).[^cite_note-FearedForce-83][^cite_note-AtStakeRegion-84][^cite_note-NoMercy-85][^cite_note-Georgy-86] The RSF perpetrated mass killings, rapes, pillage, torture, and destruction of villages. They were accused of [ethnic cleansing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing) against the [Fur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fur_people), [Masalit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masalit_people), and [Zaghawa](1467480.md) peoples.[^cite_note-NoMercy-85] Leaders of the RSF have been indicted for genocide, war crimes, and crimes against humanity by the [International Criminal Court](14880.md) (ICC),[^cite_note-FactBoxRSF-87] but Hemedti was not personally implicated in the 2003–2004 atrocities.[^cite_note-PathtoPower-82]

In 2017, a new law gave the RSF the status of an "independent security force".[^cite_note-NoMercy-85] Hemedti received several gold mines in Darfur as patronage from al-Bashir, and his personal wealth grew substantially.[^cite_note-Georgy-86][^cite_note-FactBoxRSF-87] Bashir sent RSF forces to quash a 2013 uprising in South Darfur and deployed RSF units to [fight in Yemen](46215853.md) and [Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_civil_war_\(2014%E2%80%932020\)).[^cite_note-AtStakeRegion-84] During this time, the RSF developed a working relationship with the Russian private military outfit [Wagner Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wagner_Group).[^cite_note-CNN_Paramilitary-88] RSF forces grew into the tens of thousands and obtained thousands of [armed pickup trucks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technical_\(vehicle\)) which regularly patrolled the streets of Khartoum.[^cite_note-CNN_Paramilitary-88] The Bashir regime supported the RSF and other armed groups to "[coup-proof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coup-proofing)" its power against challenges from within the regular armed forces.[^cite_note-AJLiveNews-89]

### Political transition

Main article: [Sudanese transition to democracy (2019–2021)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudanese_transition_to_democracy_\(2019%E2%80%932021\))



[Abdel Fattah al-Burhan](60480545.md), Chairman of the [Transitional Sovereignty Council](61549039.md)



Muhammad Hamdan Dagalo Musa, better known as [Hemedti](60669608.md), Commander of the [Rapid Support Forces](43053874.md)

In December 2018, protests against al-Bashir's regime began, starting the first phase of the [Sudanese revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudanese_revolution). Eight months of sustained civil disobedience were met with violent repression.[^cite_note-MEETimeline-90] In April 2019, the military (including the RSF) [ousted al-Bashir in a coup d'état](60469131.md), ending his three decades of rule; the military established the [Transitional Military Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transitional_Military_Council_\(2019\)) (TMC), a [junta](216815.md).[^cite_note-Georgy-86][^cite_note-FactBoxRSF-87][^cite_note-MEETimeline-90] Bashir was imprisoned in Khartoum; he was not turned over to the ICC, which had issued [warrants for his arrest](33596946.md).[^cite_note-DealRestoreTransition-91] [Protests calling for civilian rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019%E2%80%932022_Sudanese_protests) continued. In June 2019, the TMC's security forces, including RSF and SAF, attacked persistent demonstrators in the [Khartoum massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khartoum_massacre), with more than a hundred demonstrators killed[^cite_note-92][^cite_note-AtStakeRegion-84][^cite_note-Georgy-86][^cite_note-MEETimeline-90] and dozens raped.[^cite_note-AtStakeRegion-84] Hemedti denied orchestrating the attack.[^cite_note-Georgy-86]

In August 2019, in response to international pressure and mediation by the [African Union](10634933.md) and Ethiopia, the military agreed to share power in an interim joint civilian-military [unity government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_unity_government) (the [Transitional Sovereignty Council](61549039.md), TSC), headed by a civilian Prime Minister, [Abdalla Hamdok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdalla_Hamdok), with [elections planned for 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Next_Sudanese_general_election).[^cite_note-Abdelaziz-2023-81][^cite_note-MEETimeline-90] In October 2021, the military junta again [seized power](69110231.md) in a coup led by SAF leader al-Burhan and Hemedti. Al-Burhan became leader of the TSC government, halting [Sudan's transition to democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudanese_transition_to_democracy_\(2019%E2%80%932021\)).[^cite_note-DealRestoreTransition-91][^cite_note-Olewe-93] Several factions oppose new elections in Sudan.[^cite_note-q558-94]

### Origins of the SPLM–N and the SLM

The [Sudan Liberation Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudan_Liberation_Movement) (or Army; SLM, SLA, or SLM/A) is a rebel group in the region of Darfur, primarily of members of non-Arab ethnicities [^cite_note-95] which had been repressed by the Bashir regime.[^cite_note-96][^cite_note-97] Since 2006, the movement has split into several factions due to disagreements over the [Darfur Peace Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darfur_Peace_Agreement), with some factions joining the government in Khartoum.[^cite_note-98][^cite_note-99][^cite_note-sudan_agreement_juba-100] By 2023, the three most prominent factions were the SLM-Minnawi under [Minni Minnawi](5906722.md), the SLM-al-Nur under [Abdul Wahid al-Nur](20343335.md), and the SLM-Tambour under [Mustafa Tambour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_Tambour). The SLM-Minnawi and SLM-Tambour signed the 2020 [Juba Peace Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juba_Peace_Agreement), but the SLM-al-Nur refused and kept fighting.[^cite_note-WhosWho-101][^cite_note-102]

The SPLM–N was founded by units of the predominantly South Sudanese [Sudan People's Liberation Movement](2691327.md)/Army stationed in areas that remained in Sudan following the [South Sudanese vote for independence in 2011](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Sudanese_independence_referendum,_2011). These forces then led a rebellion in the southern states of [South Kordofan](767653.md) and [Blue Nile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Nile_\(state\)) a few months later.[^cite_note-103] In 2017, the SPLM–N split between a faction led by [Abdelaziz al-Hilu](32370982.md) and one led by [Malik Agar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malik_Agar), with al-Hilu demanding [secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism) as a condition for peace, whereas Agar did not agree with this.[^cite_note-104] During the Sudanese revolution, al-Hilu's faction declared an indefinite unilateral ceasefire.[^cite_note-victory-105] In 2020, a peace agreement was signed between the Sudanese government and Agar's faction,[^cite_note-sudan_agreement_juba-100] with Agar later joining the Transitional Sovereignty Council in Khartoum. Al-Hilu held out until he agreed to sign a separate peace agreement with the Sudanese government a few months later.[^cite_note-106] Further steps to consolidate the agreement stalled following the 2021 coup, and the al-Hilu faction instead signed an agreement with the SLM-al-Nur and the [Sudanese Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudanese_Communist_Party), agreeing to co-operate to draft a 'revolutionary charter' and remove the military from power.[^cite_note-107]

### Prelude

In the months following the 2021 coup, the already weak [Sudanese economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudanese_economy) steeply declined, fueling wide protests demanding that the junta return power to civilian authorities.[^cite_note-CrisisGroup-108] Tensions arose when al-Burhan appointed several old-guard [Islamist](15012.md) officials who had dominated politics during the al-Bashir government; Hemedti saw this as an attempt to maintain the dominance of Khartoum's traditional elite, who were hostile to Hemedti due to his ethnic background as a Darfuri Arab.[^cite_note-109] Hemedti expressed regret over his alliance with al-Burhan in the October 2021 coup.[^cite_note-Olewe-93]

Tensions between the RSF and SAF escalated in February 2023, as the RSF began to recruit members across Sudan.[^cite_note-CrisisGroup-108] Through February and early March, the RSF built up forces in the capital [Khartoum](17320.md), until a deal was brokered on 11 March and the RSF withdrew.[^cite_note-CrisisGroup-108][^cite_note-110] Negotiations were held between the SAF, RSF, and civilian leaders, but they broke down.[^cite_note-111] The chief dispute was over the integration of the RSF units into the regular SAF military: the RSF insisted on a ten-year timetable, while the army demanded two years.[^cite_note-BBC_News-112][^cite_note-MEEHowDidWeGetHere-113] Other contested issues included the status of RSF officers in the future hierarchy, and whether RSF forces should be under the command of the army chief rather than Sudan's commander-in-chief, al-Burhan.[^cite_note-114]

On 11 April 2023, RSF forces deployed near the city of [Merowe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merowe,_Sudan) as well as in [Khartoum](17320.md).[^cite_note-Sudan-115] Government forces ordered them to leave and were refused. RSF forces then took forcible control of the Soba military base south of Khartoum.[^cite_note-Sudan-115] On 13 April, RSF forces began a general mobilization, raising fears of rebellion, and the SAF declared the mobilization illegal.[^cite_note-116]

## Course of the war

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of the Sudanese civil war (2023)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Sudanese_civil_war_\(2023\)), [Timeline of the Sudanese civil war (2024)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Sudanese_civil_war_\(2024\)), [Timeline of the Sudanese civil war (2025)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Sudanese_civil_war_\(2025\)), and [Timeline of the Sudanese civil war (2026)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Sudanese_civil_war_\(2026\)).

See also: [List of engagements during the Sudanese civil war (2023–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_engagements_during_the_Sudanese_civil_war_\(2023%E2%80%93present\))

### April–May 2023

#### Battle of Khartoum

Main article: [Battle of Khartoum (2023–2025)](73563331.md)

On 15 April 2023, the RSF attacked SAF bases across Sudan, including [Khartoum](17320.md) and [its airport](4306874.md).[^cite_note-BBC_News-112][^cite_note-117] There were clashes at the headquarters of the state broadcaster [Sudan TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudan_TV), eventually captured by RSF forces.[^cite_note-BA-118] Bridges and roads in Khartoum and its hinterland were closed by the RSF.[^cite_note-Al_Jazeera-119] The next day saw an SAF counteroffensive which retook [Merowe Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merowe_Airport), near the headquarters of Sudan TV and state radio.[^cite_note-BA-118]

The Sudan Civil Aviation Authority closed the country's airspace as fighting began.[^cite_note-120] Telecommunications provider [MTN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MTN_Group) shut down Internet services, and by 23 April there was a near-total internet outage attributed to attacks on the electricity grid.[^cite_note-Siddiqui-121][^cite_note-122] International trade began to break down, with the major shipping firm [Maersk](723005.md) announcing a pause on new shipments.[^cite_note-123]

A photo of Malik Agar wearing a pink collared shirt. He is smiling at the camera.

With al-Burhan trapped in Khartoum, his deputy [Malik Agar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malik_Agar) became *de facto* leader of the Sudanese government.

[^cite_note-WhosWho-101]

[^cite_note-124]

[Hemedti](60669608.md) directed his forces to capture or kill al-Burhan, and RSF units engaged in pitched and bloody combat with the SAF [Republican Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Guard_\(Sudan\)). Al-Burhan was besieged and trapped in his headquarters in Khartoum.[^cite_note-WhosWho-101][^cite_note-125] In an interview with [Al Jazeera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Jazeera_Arabic), Hemedti accused al-Burhan and his commanders of provoking the war by scheming to bring deposed Islamist leader [Omar al-Bashir](393625.md) back to power.[^cite_note-Al_Jazeera-119] He called for the international community to intervene against al-Burhan, claiming that the RSF were fighting against radical Islamic militants.[^cite_note-126]

After a few days, SAF brought in reinforcements from the Ethiopian border.[^cite_note-AJ_Live_News-127] Despite a [ceasefire](592591.md) announced for [Eid al-Fitr](154944.md), fighting continued across the country.[^cite_note-128][^cite_note-Muted_Eid2-129] Combat was described as particularly intense along the highway from Khartoum to [Port Sudan](213572.md) and in the industrial zone of al-Bagair.[^cite_note-130] Intercommunal clashes were reported in [Blue Nile State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Nile_\(state\)) and in [Geneina](1501534.md).[^cite_note-131][^cite_note-132]

By the beginning of May, the SAF claimed to have weakened the RSF and repelled it in multiple regions.[^cite_note-133] The [Sudanese police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_enforcement_in_Sudan) [Central Reserve Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_reserve_force) joined SAF in the streets of Khartoum, claiming to have arrested several hundred RSF fighters.[^cite_note-134] SAF announced an all-out attack on RSF in Khartoum using air strikes and artillery.[^cite_note-135] Air strikes and ground offensives against RSF over the next few days damaged infrastructure but failed to dislodge RSF forces.[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137]

Following further threats from Hemedti, al-Burhan gave a public video address from his besieged headquarters, vowing to continue fighting.[^cite_note-138][^cite_note-15MayBBCreportedDagaloStatementArchived-139] On 19 May, al-Burhan officially removed Hemedti as his deputy in the Transitional Sovereignty Council, and replaced him with former rebel leader and council member [Malik Agar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malik_Agar).[^cite_note-Agar-140] With al-Burhan trapped in Khartoum, Agar became *de facto* leader of the Sudanese government, assuming responsibility for peace negotiations, international visits and the day-to-day running of the country.[^cite_note-WhosWho-101]

#### Treaty of Jeddah

Main article: [Treaty of Jeddah (2023)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Jeddah_\(2023\))

International attention to the conflict resulted in the [United Nations Human Rights Council](635790.md) (UNHRC) voting to increase monitoring of human rights abuses.[^cite_note-141] On 6 May, delegates from SAF and RSF met directly for the first time in [Jeddah](277718.md) for "pre-negotiation talks" sponsored by Saudi Arabia and the United States.[^cite_note-142] The warring sides could not agree on a ceasefire, but signed the [Treaty of Jeddah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Jeddah_\(2023\)) on 20 May, promising safe passage of civilians, protection of relief workers, and prohibition of the use of civilians as [human shields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_shields).[^cite_note-PC-143] Deadly clashes resumed in [Geneina](1501534.md), Darfur.[^cite_note-PC-143] UN [Under-Secretary-General for Humanitarian Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Under-Secretary-General_for_Humanitarian_Affairs) [Martin Griffiths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Griffiths) expressed frustration at the refusal of both sides to end the fighting.[^cite_note-Griffiths_clashes2-144]

The situation remained volatile, with both sides trading blame for attacks on churches, hospitals, and embassies.[^cite_note-church_attack-145][^cite_note-146][^cite_note-Embassy-147] Casualties mounted, particularly in Geneina, where Arab militias loyal to RSF were accused of atrocities against non-Arabs.[^cite_note-148] A temporary ceasefire was signed, but fighting persisted in Khartoum and elsewhere.[^cite_note-149] Between 28 and 97 people were reportedly killed by the RSF and Arab militias when they attacked the predominantly [Masalit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masalit_people) town of [Misterei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misterei) in West Darfur on 28 May.[^cite_note-misterei-150]

### June–September 2023



The RSF took control of the [National Museum of Sudan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Sudan) in June.

#### Continued fighting in Khartoum

As June began, tanks were deployed in battle in Khartoum.[^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152] The RSF took control of several important cultural and government buildings, including the [National Museum of Sudan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Sudan) and the [Yarmouk Military Industrial Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarmouk_Military_Industrial_Complex).[^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154] Acute food insecurity affected a significant portion of Sudan's population.[^cite_note-EEPA_06/06/23-155][^cite_note-156]

By July, al-Burhan was still trapped at Army Headquarters, and SAF sent a relief column of troops, which was ambushed and defeated, with RSF claiming it had killed hundreds of soldiers and captured 90 vehicles, along with the column's commander.[^cite_note-157] SAF increased the intensity of their airstrikes and artillery bombardment, often killing dozens of civilians in each strike.[^cite_note-158][^cite_note-aj32-159][^cite_note-f24strike-160] RSF shelling also intensified, with many civilian casualties.[^cite_note-161][^cite_note-10dead-162]

Heavy fighting continued through August across Khartoum. RSF laid siege to SAF's Armoured Corps base, breaching its defences and taking control of surrounding neighbourhoods.[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164] SAF also made offensives, with the RSF-controlled [Republican Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Palace,_Khartoum) and Yarmouk Complex coming under SAF air bombardment. An offensive was launched against Yarmouk, but this was beaten back by RSF reinforcements.[^cite_note-palace3-165] One of the few remaining bridges between Khartoum and [Khartoum North](3145079.md) was also destroyed by SAF to impede RSF maneuvers.[^cite_note-166]

On 24 August an SAF operation successfully rescued al-Burhan from his besieged Army Headquarters, allowing him to head to [Port Sudan](213572.md) and hold a cabinet meeting there.[^cite_note-167][^cite_note-168]

#### Diplomatic efforts

Ceasefires between the warring parties were announced but often violated, with SAF and RSF blaming each other, while the Sudanese government took action against international envoys.[^cite_note-169] The Saudi embassy in Khartoum was attacked and an orphanage was evacuated amid the chaos.[^cite_note-170] Sudan faced diplomatic strains with Egypt, causing difficulty for Sudanese refugees seeking entry.[^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172]

Al-Burhan, free for the first time since the start of the war, flew to Egypt to meet president [Abdel Fattah al-Sisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdel_Fattah_al-Sisi).[^cite_note-173] Al-Burhan then made a tour of numerous countries, heading to South Sudan, Qatar, Eritrea, Turkey, and Uganda.[^cite_note-174] He proceeded to [New York](645042.md) as head of the Sudanese delegation to the 78th [United Nations General Assembly](31957.md), where he urged the international community to declare the RSF a terrorist organization.[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176]

#### SPLM–N (Al-Hilu) involvement

The [Abdelaziz al-Hilu](32370982.md) faction of the [Sudan People's Liberation Movement–North](30884032.md) (SPLM–N) broke a long-standing ceasefire in June, attacking SAF units in [Kadugli](3956578.md), [Kurmuk](5985370.md) and [Dalang](77702874.md), the latter coinciding with an attack by the RSF. SAF claimed to have repelled the attacks,[^cite_note-reserve-177][^cite_note-SPLMN-26] while the rebels claimed to have attacked in retaliation for the death of one of their soldiers, and vowed to free the region from "military occupation".[^cite_note-victory-105] More than 35,000 were displaced by the fighting.[^cite_note-victory-105] Speculation arose as to whether al-Hilu's attacks were part of an unofficial alliance with RSF, or an attempt to strengthen al-Hilu's position in future negotiations.[^cite_note-178] Civil society organizations supporting SPLM–N claimed its operations sought to protect civilians from possible RSF attacks.[^cite_note-179]

Al-Hilu's faction launched further offensives in July, moving into [South Kordofan](767653.md) and gaining control of several SAF bases.[^cite_note-terrain-180][^cite_note-terrain22-181] SAF countered with heavy bombardment of SPLM–N positions.[^cite_note-terrain-180] Further attacks by SPLM-N largely petered out after this, with a September assault on Kadugli being pushed back by SAF.[^cite_note-182]

Two years later in February 2025, SPLM–N (Al-Hilu) would officially ally with the RSF, signing the *Sudan Founding Charter* of the RSF-led [Government of Peace and Unity](79260861.md).[^cite_note-4killed-27]

#### Darfur front

In Darfur, fighting and bloodshed were particularly fierce around the city of [Geneina](1501534.md), where hundreds died and extensive destruction occurred.[^cite_note-real_hell-183] RSF forces engaged in frequent violence against the [Masalit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masalit) of Geneina, leading to accusations of [ethnic cleansing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing).[^cite_note-184] On 4 August, RSF claimed it had taken full control of [Central Darfur](34420004.md).[^cite_note-185]

A UN investigation in Darfur discovered numerous mass graves of Masalit civilians.[^cite_note-mass_grave-186] RSF and Arab militias were additionally accused of killing lawyers, human rights monitors, doctors and non-Arab tribal leaders.[^cite_note-187] The governor of [West Darfur](767645.md), [Khamis Abakar](74121008.md), was abducted and killed in June, hours after accusing RSF of genocide and calling for international intervention in a TV interview.[^cite_note-governor-188] SAF conducted indiscriminate airstrikes against Darfur that killed many civilians, especially in Nyala.[^cite_note-189][^cite_note-190]

Tribal and rebel groups in Darfur began to declare allegiance to one of the two warring parties. A faction of the Darfur-based [Sudan Liberation Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudan_Liberation_Movement) led by [Mustafa Tambour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_Tambour) (SLM-T) joined in support of SAF.[^cite_note-SLM-16] In contrast, the controversial [Tamazuj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamazuj) rebel group allied with RSF, joined by the leaders of seven Arab tribes, including that of Hemedti.[^cite_note-tamazuj-191][^cite_note-192]

As September arrived, both sides made offensives in Darfur. RSF took control of several towns in [West Darfur](767645.md) and attacked the market of [El Fasher](3956787.md), the capital of [North Darfur](767666.md).[^cite_note-antonov-193] SAF offensives saw success in [Central Darfur](34420004.md), retaking parts of [Zalingei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zalingei) from RSF.[^cite_note-fires-194] Fighting also began to spill over into [North Kordofan](767669.md), with SAF attacking RSF positions in the state capital of [El-Obeid](1481380.md) and clashes over the town of [Um Rawaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Um_Rawaba).[^cite_note-195] Both sides made withdrawals in late September, with RSF retreating from Um Rawaba while SAF withdrew from [Tawila](74157994.md).[^cite_note-196][^cite_note-197]

### October–December 2023

#### SAF collapse in Darfur



By the end of November, [El Fasher](3956787.md) was the last of the five state capitals in Darfur under SAF control.

By October 2023, SAF in Darfur was experiencing acute supply shortages due to RSF sieges, and had failed to utilize its air superiority to stem RSF advances.[^cite_note-Zalingei-198] On 26 October, RSF [captured Nyala](73727776.md), Sudan's [fourth-largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Sudan), after seizing control of SAF 16th Infantry Division headquarters.[^cite_note-199] The fall of Nyala, a strategic city with an international airport and border connections to Central Africa, allowed RSF to receive international supplies more easily and freed up its forces for other campaigns.[^cite_note-200] RSF troops proceeded to march into [Zalingei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zalingei), the capital of Central Darfur, as the SAF 21st Infantry Division fled without a fight.[^cite_note-201]

In Geneina, reports emerged that tribal elders were attempting to broker the peaceful surrender of the SAF garrison.[^cite_note-DarfurCampaign-202] SAF rejected the proposal, raising fears of an imminent RSF assault and causing civilians to flee across the border into Chad.[^cite_note-Al-Geneina-57] RSF besieged the headquarters of the SAF 15th Infantry Division in Geneina, giving the garrison a six-hour ultimatum to surrender.[^cite_note-203] The base was captured two days later, when the 15th withdrew and fled to Chad.[^cite_note-Chad-204] The hundreds left behind were taken prisoner and paraded in RSF media with signs of abuse.[^cite_note-Chad-204] Witnesses later reported mass atrocities by the RSF shortly after seizing the city, with a local rebel group claiming up to [2,000 people massacred](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ardamata_massacre) in the suburb of [Ardamata](75268036.md).[^cite_note-Ardamata-205] With Geneina's fall, [Ed Daein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ed_Daein) and El Fasher were the last remaining capitals in Darfur under SAF control, with both cities under heavy RSF pressure.[^cite_note-DarfurCampaign-202][^cite_note-Chad-204]

The RSF stormed and plundered the town of [Umm Keddada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umm_Keddada), east of El Fasher, after the SAF garrison withdrew.[^cite_note-Ardamata-205] SAF troops in El Fasher itself were reported to be running low on food, water, and medicine due to the siege, and external forces noted SAF seemed incapable of stopping the RSF advance.[^cite_note-Fashir-206][^cite_note-207] Ed Daein fell in the early hours of 21 November, with RSF forces seizing the headquarters of the SAF 20th Infantry Division.[^cite_note-208] SAF garrisons in East Darfur subsequently abandoned their positions and withdrew, allowing the RSF to occupy the area.[^cite_note-209] In response to RSF gains in Darfur and subsequent abuses, the [Justice and Equality Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_and_Equality_Movement), [Sudan Liberation Movement/Army (Minnawi)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudan_Liberation_Movement), and other smaller rebel factions renounced their neutrality and declared war on the RSF.[^cite_note-slm-jem-17]

#### Peace negotiations stall

International attempts to negotiate peace had largely been dormant since the failed Treaty of Jeddah, but in late October 2023, the RSF and SAF met once more in Jeddah.[^cite_note-210] These talks again failed, with neither side willing to commit to a ceasefire, though they again promised to open channels for humanitarian aid.[^cite_note-211] On 3 December 2023, negotiations were halted when neither party fulfilled this promise.[^cite_note-212]

The East African [Intergovernmental Authority on Development](1921406.md) (IGAD) hosted a peace summit in early December, though SAF had previously accused Kenyan president [William Ruto](2577967.md) of supporting RSF.[^cite_note-213] IGAD's talks appeared to make some progress, with Hemedti and al-Burhan agreeing to meet in person at some point in the future.[^cite_note-214]

#### RSF crossing of the Nile

Further information: [Battle of Wad Madani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Wad_Madani)

RSF attacked the town of Wad Ashana in North Kordofan on 1 October along a key commercial route.[^cite_note-215][^cite_note-216] In West Kordofan, an uptick in fighting was reported, with RSF assaulting a major oil field in Baleela, south of [Al-Fulah](39116066.md).[^cite_note-217] Geolocated footage showed RSF fighters celebrating around Baleela Airport after allegedly capturing it.[^cite_note-Baleela-218] The Battle of Khartoum continued, with RSF seizing the town of al-Aylafoun, southeast of the capital, on 6 October, with RAF seizing key oil infrastructure.[^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220] By late October, RSF controlled most of Khartoum but had failed to seize key military bases, while al-Burhan's government had largely relocated to Port Sudan.[^cite_note-221]



The [Shambat Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shambat_Bridge) in Khartoum was destroyed on 11 November.

[^cite_note-Shambat-222]

RSF sought to capitalize on its gains by stepping up attacks on SAF positions in Khartoum and Omdurman. Days of fighting culminated in the destruction of the [Shambat Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shambat_Bridge) connecting Khartoum North to Omdurman over the Nile; the bridge's destruction severed a critical RSF supply route.[^cite_note-Shambat-222] This effectively cut off RSF forces in Omdurman, giving the SAF a strategic advantage.[^cite_note-223] In an attempt to gain a new crossing over the Nile and supply its forces in Omdurman, RSF attacked the [Jebel Aulia Dam](29505035.md) in the village of [Jabal Awliya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jabal_Awliya).[^cite_note-224] As the dam could not be destroyed without flooding Khartoum, its capture would give RSF a path over the Nile the SAF could not easily remove. A week-long battle commenced over the dam and village, ending in RSF victory on 20-21 November.[^cite_note-JebDam-225][^cite_note-226]

On 5 December, local militias along with RSF soldiers attacked SPLM–N (al-Hilu) forces in the village of Tukma, southeast of Dalang in South Kordofan, killing four people and destroying the village.[^cite_note-4killed-27] RSF leadership, not wanting hostilities with the then-neutral al-Hilu faction to escalate, condemned this attack, minimizing it as "tribal violence".[^cite_note-Qadarif-227] On 8 December, the RSF entered [Gedaref State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Qadarif_State) for the first time.[^cite_note-Qadarif-227]

Pushing south from their gains around Jebel Aulia and Khartoum, RSF forces began to move into [Gezira State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gezira_State) on 15 December, advancing toward its capital [Wad Madani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wad_Madani).[^cite_note-228][^cite_note-229] Elsewhere in Gezira the RSF made major gains, taking control of the city of [Rufaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rufaa_\(city\)) in the state's east and entering the [Butana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butana) region.[^cite_note-HantoobBridge-230] After several days of fighting the RSF seized the Hantoob Bridge on Wad Madani's eastern outskirts, crossing the [Blue Nile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Nile) and entering the city.[^cite_note-HantoobBridge-230] The Sudanese Army put up little resistance in Wad Madani itself, the 1st Division withdrawing from the city as the RSF took over.[^cite_note-WadMadaniMonitor-231]

The fall of Wad Madani was viewed as a major blow to SAF, as it dramatically widened the frontline and opened up large parts of the country to potential RSF offensives.[^cite_note-WadMadaniMonitor-231] The city's fall allowed RSF to capture most of Gezira and to make inroads in [White Nile State](767622.md), capturing the town of [El Geteina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Geteina).[^cite_note-GeziraOffensive-232] Within a few days RSF fighters had advanced to within 25 km of [Sennar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sennar), the largest city in [Sennar State](767672.md).[^cite_note-GeziraOffensive-232] Over the next few weeks RSF forces ventured into rural areas of [Al Qadarif State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Qadarif_State) and [River Nile State](767659.md), without establishing a significant presence. In Sennar State the RSF made some further minor advances but had not attacked Sennar City by the year's end.[^cite_note-233]

Amid the deteriorating situation, the SAF were reported to be arming civilians while government officials in the east called on the population to mobilize.[^cite_note-234] Al-Burhan gave a widely promoted public speech to soldiers in [Red Sea State](767604.md), promising to arm civilian militias to fight the RSF and to fight against 'colonialism', which was viewed by observers as a reference to Emirati support for the RSF.[^cite_note-RwandaMemorial-235]

### January–April 2024

Map of Sudanese Civil War 2024

Map of the Sudanese Civil War in March 2024

By January 2024, the war's economic costs had surpassed all prior armed conflicts since Sudanese independence in 1956 due to extensive destruction of infrastructure, particularly in urban areas such as the capital city of [Khartoum](17320.md).[^cite_note-236]

#### Hemedti travels abroad

Following the fall of Wad Madani, efforts by IGAD to negotiate a ceasefire made progress as the SAF's weakened position made them more eager to enter talks. Whereas previously opposition from Islamist political groups to negotiation had prevented al-Burhan from committing to a specific date, now both he and Hemedti agreed to meet on 28 December.[^cite_note-WadMadaniMonitor-231][^cite_note-237] A day before the meeting was due, it was cancelled as Hemedti recanted his desire to attend.[^cite_note-238]

Instead the RSF leader went on a diplomatic tour, travelling on a chartered Emirati jet and meeting with several African national leaders.[^cite_note-Signals-239] One visit that was particularly promoted was his visit to [Rwanda](25645.md), where he met with Rwandan president [Paul Kagame](393593.md) and visited the [Kigali Genocide Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kigali_Genocide_Memorial).[^cite_note-RwandaMemorial-235] On the tour Hemedti also met with former Prime Minister Hamdok and his [Taqaddum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taqaddum_\(Sudan\)) organisation in [Addis Ababa](52593.md), with the RSF agreeing in a declaration negotiated with the Taqaddum to release political prisoners, open up humanitarian aid corridors and negotiate further with the SAF.[^cite_note-240] This tour was regarded by observers as an attempt by Hemedti to portray himself as the leader of Sudan and improve his international image, as his reputation had been severely damaged since the fall of Wad Madani due to large-scale looting by RSF fighters.[^cite_note-Signals-239]

On 5 January, al-Burhan vowed to continue the war against the RSF and rejected the latest peace efforts, declaring that war crimes committed by the RSF precluded negotiation.[^cite_note-241] On 14 January, both Hemedti and Burhan received official invitations from IGAD to attend its upcoming summit on 18 January. Hemedti accepted the invitation, but Burhan refused. On 16 January, the Sudanese government suspended its ties with IGAD, accusing the body of violating Sudan's sovereignty. This effectively marked the end of IGAD's efforts to mediate peace talks.[^cite_note-242]

#### Fighting in Kordofan and Gezira

As 2024 began, the RSF made attacks into [South Kordofan](767653.md), defeating SAF forces in the town of [Habila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habila) in the [Nuba Mountains](1139195.md) and pushing toward [Dalang](33104297.md).[^cite_note-243] On 7 January the RSF attacked SAF positions in Dalang, meeting fierce resistance from the army and civilian militias.[^cite_note-RwandaMemorial-235][^cite_note-Dalang-244] During the fighting the SPLM–N (al-Hilu) entered the city, taking control of several neighbourhoods. SPLM–N forces proceeded to attack the RSF, and the paramilitary retreated from the city.[^cite_note-RSFDalangDefeat-245] RSF fighters withdrawing from Dalang entered the city of [Muglad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muglad) in [West Kordofan](767647.md), easily taking control as the city had no organised SAF presence. West Kordofan had been relatively free of fighting for several months due to a local truce brokered by leaders of the [Messiria tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiria_tribe), but as tensions escalated rumours spread that the RSF were planning an attack on the encircled city of [Babanusa](22085627.md) and the Sudanese Army's 22nd Infantry Division garrisoning it.[^cite_note-RSFDalangDefeat-245]

In January 2024, the RSF focused on consolidating its gains in [Gezira State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gezira_State). Fighting was reported on 17 January east of [El Manaqil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Manaqil), the last major town not under RSF control. The SAF delivered weapons to the city by helicopter, including selectively distributing them among civilians in the town, attempting to bolster its defences. Sudan's [National Intelligence and Security Service (NISS)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Intelligence_Service_\(Sudan\)) selectively recruited and armed civilians based on perceived loyalty.[^cite_note-246] On 24 January 2024, the RSF launched an attack on Babanusa after encircling the city for months. By 25 January, the RSF gained control of the city centre and entered the headquarters of the 22nd infantry division.[^cite_note-247]

Until March 2024, the RSF maintained its position in Gezira State but was unable to break through.[^cite_note-sudanwarmonitor.com-248] The RSF recruited in Gezira State to try to capture territory in El-Gadarif from the SAF.[^cite_note-249] The [JEM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_and_Equality_Movement), which has allied with the SAF, helped the SAF build up its forces in [El-Gadarif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El-Gadarif) for a counteroffensive to try to retake [Wad Madani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wad_Madani). In April 2024, the SAF and its allies began the counteroffensive, attacking from the east and west of Wad Madani in an attempt to retake it.[^cite_note-250] Clashes were reported in Al-Madina Arab on 15 April.[^cite_note-251]

In December 2024, the SAF launched an offensive in southern Gezira. The SAF were able to make small progress, which involved recapturing the town of Wad el-Haddad, a town on the border of Sennar State. It also was able to recapture [Um al-Qura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Um_al-Qura,_Sudan?action=edit\&redlink=1), but the RSF reoccupied the village.[^cite_note-252]

In January 2025, the SAF made the first major military operation of 2025. The Sudanese Army was able to make large gains in Gezira and some gains in North Kordofan. On 8 January, the SAF had recaptured Haj-Abdallah after a tense battle that inflicted losses on the RSF. The SAF stated that seven RSF vehicles were destroyed.[^cite_note-253] A day later, the SAF attacked RSF positions in Al-Shabarga in the southeastern part of the state, led by field commander Bassam Abu Satour, leading to the RSF's withdrawal and the SAF recapturing the city, while in the western part of the state, the SAF took control of the villages Mahla, Tahla, and Al-Kumar Al-Jaaliyeen.[^cite_note-254] On 10 January, the SAF recaptured Um al-Qura while the Sudan Shield Forces took Wad al-Abyad.[^cite_note-255] These successful offensives led to the SAF [retaking control of Wad Madani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Wad_Madani) on 11 January from three fronts.



A sketch map of Omdurman with Khartoum and [Khartoum North](3145079.md) (Bahri). The [White Nile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Nile) flowing from the south is joined by the [Blue Nile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Nile) flowing from the east.

After advancing in Gezira and Khartoum, the SAF launched a military operation in North Kordofan for the first time, after being on the defensive in Darfur and Kordofan from the start of the war. The SAF's "Sayyad Force", captured the entirety of the [Umm Ruwaba](26735189.md) district.[^cite_note-PP-256]

By the start of February, the SAF had recaptured Al-Hasaheisa, [Tambul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tambul), and [Rufa'a](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rufaa_\(city\)). This left the RSF in control of only northwestern [Gezira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gezira_State).[^cite_note-PP-256]

The SAF then liberated the town of [Er Rahad](5775629.md) on 19 February, and by 23 February, the SAF had lifted the almost two-year [siege of El Obeid](74051914.md).[^cite_note-257][^cite_note-258]

#### SAF gains in Omdurman

The SAF gained ground in Omdurman in February 2024, linking up their forces in the northern part of the city and relieving a 10-month siege of their forces in the city centre. The SAF also took control of the [Al-Hilal Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Hilal_Stadium).[^cite_note-BreaksSiege-259] The Omdurman front was the first area in Sudan where the SAF has carried out a sustained offensive operation and represents the first breakthrough for the SAF.[^cite_note-BreaksSiege-259]

On 12 March, the SAF defeated an attempted RSF counteroffensive in Omdurman and took control of the headquarters of the [Sudan National Broadcasting Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudan_National_Broadcasting_Corporation).[^cite_note-260] The RSF maintained its control of Khartoum and continued to threaten Khartoum North.[^cite_note-261]

By April 2024, fighting in Khartoum State was still ongoing, with the RSF in control of the southern and western parts of Omdurman and the SAF in control of the northern and eastern parts of Omdurman, with the RSF controlling the majority of Khartoum and Khartoum North. The SAF continued to prepare an offensive to relieve its surrounded bases in [Khartoum North](3145079.md).[^cite_note-262]

As of March 2025, SAF controls the majority of the city. On 29 March, SAF forces announced the control of the Libya Market in Omdurman and seized weapons and equipment left behind by the RSF.[^cite_note-263] On the same day, SAF launched new offensives into the city of [Ombadda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ombadda), west of Omdurman.[^cite_note-264]

### April–December 2024

#### Fighting in Darfur

Main article: [Siege of El Fasher](73754779.md)

On 15 April, during the [Siege of El Fasher](73754779.md), at least nine civilians were killed in a renewed offensive by the [RSF](43053874.md) on the city of [El Fasher](3956787.md) in North Darfur.[^cite_note-265] The [Joint Darfur Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Darfur_Force) declared war on the RSF and allied with the SAF.[^cite_note-266][^cite_note-267]

The fighting in El Fasher diverted SAF resources from other areas, hampering planned counter-offensives to retake Khartoum and Wad Madani. In particular, the SAF has been using its limited aviation resources to carry out airstrikes in North Darfur and resupply El Fasher using airdrops.[^cite_note-268]

From April 2024, the conflict had been escalating in El Fasher, while the civilians remained trapped with no safety or food. In a June 2024 report, the International Crisis Group said the intensifying battle could lead to mass slaughter, and that there was a need for all sides to de-escalate. The report said the UN and the US should broker the de-escalation and must put pressure on the RSF and its main supporters, including the United Arab Emirates. Crisis Group said the UAE should push the RSF to stand down, and urged all parties to allow the civilians to flee, open the region for aid delivery and resume national peace talks.[^cite_note-269]

On 14 June 2024, the SAF announced that it had killed [Ali Yaqoub Gibril](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Yaqoub_Gibril), a top RSF commander, in El Fasher. The United States had sanctioned Yagoub in May 2024 for endangering civilians in Darfur.[^cite_note-270] In June 2024, *The New York Times* reported that more than 40 villages had been burned in El Fasher since April 2024.[^cite_note-271]

#### Fighting in Kordofan

As of May 2024, fighting is ongoing in [Babanusa](22085627.md), West Kordofan. The RSF are conducting an offensive to attempt to take control of West Kordofan.[^cite_note-272] Fighting was also reported in North Kordofan.[^cite_note-273]

On 20 June 2024, the RSF captured [Al-Fulah](39116066.md), the capital of [West Kordofan](767647.md), after the SAF withdrew from the city after several hours of fighting. The SAF retreated to Babanusa, its one remaining base in Kordofan.[^cite_note-274]

#### Fighting along the Nile

Further information: [Sennar offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sennar_offensive)



Map of the [Blue Nile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Nile) and [White Nile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Nile) rivers

In May 2024, the RSF launched attacks against the SAF between Khartoum State and [River Nile State](767659.md), as well as in [White Nile State](767622.md) near the border with Gezira State. The SAF prepared its forces in River Nile State, ahead of a potential invasion of Khartoum Bahri.[^cite_note-275] In June 2024, the RSF were still in control of Khartoum and Khartoum North, though the SAF controlled one enclave in each that it supplies by airdrop.[^cite_note-276]

In late June 2024, the RSF began an assault in the areas surrounding the city of Sennar. RSF forces struck out to the west of the city, causing the SAF to bring in reinforcements in anticipation of an attack on Sennar itself.[^cite_note-Blitzkrieg-277] Instead RSF forces avoided Sennar and attacked south towards [Singa](37739532.md), the capital of Sennar State, capturing the lightly defended town on 29 June.[^cite_note-Blitzkrieg-277][^cite_note-278] This prevented the SAF from reinforcing Sennar from the south, placing the city under increased pressure.[^cite_note-Blitzkrieg-277]

Following the fall of Singa, SAF resistance collapsed across much of southern Sennar, which led to the RSF occupying the towns of [Dinder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinder,_Sudan), Mazmoun and Wad an-Nail with minimal SAF resistance.[^cite_note-279] A united force consisting of the [Gedaref](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gedaref)-based 2nd SAF Infantry Division and a battalion of the JEM assaulted and retook Dinder on 1 July, but were driven out again by the RSF over the next few days.[^cite_note-280]

On 20 July, the RSF announced the death of Brigadier General Abdel Rahman Al-Bishi, its head of operations in Sennar and [Blue Nile States](767625.md), with Sudanese media reporting that he had been killed in a SAF airstrike.[^cite_note-281]

On 3 August, the RSF launched its first attack on Blue Nile State since the beginning of the war, with the group and the SAF contesting control over Al-Tadamun.[^cite_note-282] On 15 August 2024, the [Galgani massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galgani_massacre) was carried out by the RSF, which killed at least 108 people,[^cite_note-283] including at least 24 women and children.[^cite_note-284]

#### SAF offensives

As September 2024 came, for the first time since the start of the war the balance of power seemed to be tipping towards the SAF. On 26 September, the SAF launched a major offensive against RSF positions in Khartoum.[^cite_note-285] The attack on the city came from three fronts striking from the south, east and west of the capital.[^cite_note-WS-286] SAF airstrikes, which killed four and wounded 14, began at dawn followed by clashes within the city. The SAF reportedly captured three key bridges connecting Khartoum to other nearby cities, including the [Omdurman Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omdurman_Bridge) which had previously acted as a line of separation between government and RSF control.[^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288] Faced with an elusive enemy, the SAF became bogged down in [urban fighting](319027.md), with RSF snipers routinely paralyzing infantry advances.[^cite_note-WS-286]

October 2024 was the deadliest month for Sudanese civilians since the war began. In Khartoum, the RSF have relentlessly shelled areas controlled by the SAF, which has amounted to daily indiscriminate bombardments of civilian areas. Escalating SAF [airstrikes](323167.md) on RSF positions have caused dozens of civilian deaths.[^cite_note-WS-286] In October 2024, the SAF also launched counteroffensives in the states of [Sennar](767672.md) and [Gezira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gezira_State), which were successfully recaptured from the RSF.[^cite_note-289] Starting on 20 October 2024, the RSF carried out the [2024 eastern Gezira State massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_eastern_Gezira_State_massacres), which killed at least 300 people and wounded at least 200 more.[^cite_note-290]

According to a report by the French newspaper *[Le Monde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Monde)*, as of November 2024, the war in Sudan has possibly entered its most dangerous phase since it began in April 2023. Both the SAF and RSF have officially ruled out settling the civil war through negotiations, with the only option on the table being [total war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_war). During the recent [rainy season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wet_season) which brought a lull in the fighting, each side rearmed and restructured their forces.[^cite_note-WS-286] Many ordinary Sudanese, extending to the most serious critics of the SAF, have increasingly supported the SAF in response to RSF war crimes and atrocities. The SAF has become increasingly dependent on [Islamist](15012.md) networks, as these movements have mobilized many civilians from [popular resistance brigades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_Resistance_of_Sudan). The [Al-Bara' ibn Malik Battalion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Bara'_ibn_Malik_Battalion) in particular is presently fighting on the [Khartoum](17320.md) front lines against the RSF and has consequently gained popularity.[^cite_note-291] On 23 November, the SAF retook Singa following an offensive.[^cite_note-292][^cite_note-293]

### 2025

#### Liberation of Khartoum

The SAF retook [Wad Madani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wad_Madani), the capital of [Gezira State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gezira_State) on 11 January.[^cite_note-294] On 8 February 2025, the SAF regained control of nearly all of Khartoum North as it intensified its offensive, and was preparing to retake the capital of Khartoum itself.[^cite_note-295] On 24 February, the RSF claimed responsibility for downing a Russian-made [Ilyushin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilyushin) aircraft in Nyala. Meanwhile, the RSF declared a rival government in [Nairobi](21482.md), the capital of Kenya, which the SAF-aligned administration refused to recognize.[^cite_note-296][^cite_note-297]

On 20 March, the SAF announced it was within 500 metres of the Presidential Palace[^cite_note-298] and captured it on the next day.[^cite_note-299] On 22 March, the SAF also recaptured the headquarters of the [Central Bank of Sudan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Bank_of_Sudan) and the [General Intelligence Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Intelligence_Service_\(Sudan\)) in Khartoum.[^cite_note-300] It also retook [Tuti Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuti_Island), situated at the confluence of the [Blue Nile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Nile) and the [White Nile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Nile), after advancing through the [Tuti Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuti_Bridge).[^cite_note-301] On 26 March, they retook Khartoum International Airport and Jebel Aulia, regarded as the RSF's last stronghold in the capital,[^cite_note-302] with al-Burhan proclaiming the liberation of Khartoum later in the day.[^cite_note-303] On 20 May, the SAF announced the clearing of [Khartoum State](767612.md) from the RSF.[^cite_note-304][^cite_note-305]

#### Fall of El Fasher

Further information: [Siege of El Fasher](73754779.md) and [El Fasher massacre](81461601.md)

Earlier in November 2024, the SAF reportedly shifted tactics: withdrawing from outer bases to lure RSF forces into trap engagements, especially in the southern axis of El Fasher.[^cite_note-306] From December 2024 onwards the RSF were also mobilising additional fighters across Darfur for a concerted operation on the city. Reports mentioned mobilisation of some 200 fighters from Central Darfur, the recruitment of foreign mercenaries and the RSF staging air and ground assaults on the city's hospitals and camps.[^cite_note-307]

In April, the RSF launched a major offensive in [North Darfur](767666.md), aiming to capture El Fasher, the last state capital in the region under SAF control. Beginning on 11 April, RSF ground and aerial assaults struck El Fasher and surrounding displacement camps, including [Zamzam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zamzam_Refugee_Camp) and Abu Shouk. By 13 April, the RSF claimed control of Zamzam camp after intense fighting that left over 200 civilians dead, including children and aid workers.[^cite_note-ZC-308][^cite_note-309] The SAF denied RSF accusations of militarizing the camp, while rights groups documented widespread abuses by RSF fighters, including targeted killings and sexual violence.[^cite_note-Sudan-2025b-68][^cite_note-ZC-308]

Artillery fire by RSF howitzers preceded ground attacks deep into the southern and northwestern sectors of the city. On 30 June 2025, an RSF-launched howitzer barrage targeted SAF forward positions in southern El Fasher, which the SAF responded to with artillery and drone strikes.[^cite_note-310] During this exchange, civilian neighbourhoods were also caught in the fighting; one hospital source reported injuries to civilians though exact numbers were not confirmed.[^cite_note-311]

On 19 September, RSF combat columns pushed toward SAF positions near the "Super Camp" southwest of the city, after which SAF sources reported defensive operations in the neighbourhoods of Al-Nasrat, Al-Shorfa and Al-Qubba.[^cite_note-Heavy_Fighting_in_El_Fasher_as_RSF_Advances_Toward_Army_Positions-2025-312] The RSF had already throughout the siege of El Fasher erected earthen berms encircling the city from the north, west and east, forming a kill-box environment that severely restricted SAF resupply and civilian movement.[^cite_note-313]

By August and September 2025, the siege conditions had degraded SAF supply lines significantly; SAF units admitted to hunger, low morale and desertion as food, ammunition and medical logistics dwindled.[^cite_note-314] RSF penetrations into the city's periphery, especially blocks 16 and 17 of Abu Shouk and Naivasha market area, indicated that the outer ring of SAF control was collapsing.[^cite_note-Heavy_Fighting_in_El_Fasher_as_RSF_Advances_Toward_Army_Positions-2025-312]

In October, the RSF took complete control of the headquarters of the 6th Infantry Division, the main base of the SAF in El Fasher.[^cite_note-315] On 28 October, General al-Burhan confirmed that the SAF had withdrawn from El Fasher, confirming RSF control over the city. In October 2025, humanitarian workers and local officials reported that [more than 2,500 civilians were summarily executed by the RSF](81461601.md) following the fall of the city.[^cite_note-316] The [WHO](33583.md) reported that more than 460 patients and their companions were killed inside the city's last functioning hospital.[^cite_note-317] Analysis of satellite imagery suggested that the RSF disposed of tens of thousands of bodies through burial and incineration to cover up mass killings.[^cite_note-318] As of December 2025, some estimates place the total deaths from the El Fasher massacre between 60,000 and 68,000+.[^cite_note-319] Kholood Khair estimated that 100,000 people were killed in the "genocidal violence" but noted that there were no official figures due to the lack of governance in the region.[^cite_note-inswor-320]

#### Kordofan offensives

On 1 May, the RSF announced that they had taken control of [En Nahud](5744176.md), a strategic city in [West Kordofan](767647.md) that was previously used by the SAF to send forces to Darfur.[^cite_note-321] Despite initial setbacks, Al-Khiwai in West Kordofan was retaken by the SAF on 11 May,[^cite_note-322] and on 13 May, the town of Al-Hamadi in [South Kordofan](767653.md), an administrative hub for the [Hawazma tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawazma_tribe), was also retaken by the SAF, alongside some strongholds in southern Omdurman previously held by the RSF, such as the Al-Jami'a neighbourhood and all of the Al-Shaqla neighbourhood.[^cite_note-323]

On 1 December, the RSF announced that they had taken control of [Babanusa](22085627.md), the last SAF-held city in [West Kordofan](767647.md), after a [two-year siege](75934201.md).[^cite_note-324] On 8 December, the RSF seized the [Heglig](6796525.md) oil field[^cite_note-325] after the Sudanese Army withdrew across the southern border, as they feared fighting to defend the oilfield would see it destroyed. Production at Heglig was at about 20,000 barrels per day, significantly down from the pre-war level of 64,000.[^cite_note-326] Upon arriving in [Unity State](767617.md), South Sudan, the Sudanese soldiers were disarmed by the [South Sudan People's Defence Forces](19196675.md). Lt. Gen. Johnson Olony, South Sudan's Deputy Chief of Defence Forces for Mobilisation and Disarmament, said: "We received them because they are our brothers". He also said this was coordinated between President [Salva Kiir Mayardit](2363203.md) of South Sudan and Abdel Fatah al-Burhan. Olony also said his army would take Heglig from the RSF "... to prioritize regional stability."[^cite_note-327]

#### Fighting in border regions

On 5 May 2025, the RSF attacked [Port Sudan](213572.md) for the first time using drones.[^cite_note-328]

On 19 May, the SAF took Wadi al-Atrun in Al-Malha, located on a strategic road linking [Northern State](767636.md) and North Darfur.[^cite_note-329] On 21 May, the SAF said it had cleared [White Nile State](767622.md) of the RSF.[^cite_note-330] On 23 May, the SAF announced the capture of the strategic city of [Dibebad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dibebad) in South Kordofan.[^cite_note-331]

On 10 June, part of the border triangle linking Sudan, Libya, and Egypt at [Gabal El Uweinat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabal_El_Uweinat) was attacked by the RSF and [Libyan National Army](35457665.md) (LNA), led by [Khalifa Haftar](31227894.md), which struck directly into Sudanese territory controlled by the SAF. On 11 June, the RSF announced that they had entirely occupied the area; The SAF retreated and condemned the LNA for the strikes.[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-NABDLibya-58] By 16 June, the RSF had captured the entire Sudan-Libya border.[^cite_note-333] On 22 June, after several days of fighting, the SPLM–N (al-Hilu) cut off the road connecting [Kadugli](3956578.md) and [Dalang](33104297.md), placing them under siege. On 26 June, SAF recaptured Malken in the [Blue Nile](767625.md) front as part of efforts to increase the pressure toward RSF. The SAF launched a massive counterattack on 28 June, reopening the road between Dalang and Kadugli.[^cite_note-334]

#### Diplomacy

Diplomatic efforts to broker a truce were active in early 2025 but largely unsuccessful. Various proposed peace talks held in London, Washington, and Geneva failed to produce a lasting agreement.[^cite_note-335]

In April 2025, a British-led conference in London attempted to establish a contact group to restart negotiations, but the effort faltered when key Arab states (especially Egypt, Saudi Arabia, and the UAE) refused to endorse a joint [communiqué](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_release).[^cite_note-Wintour-2025-336] While the UK, EU, and [African Union](10634933.md) pushed for a ceasefire and political roadmap, the regional powers prioritized different outcomes.[^cite_note-Wintour-2025-336][^cite_note-337]

By September 2025, the United States together with Saudi Arabia, Egypt, and the UAE—known collectively as the "Quad"—presented a formal peace plan. The roadmap proposed a three-month humanitarian truce, to be followed by a permanent ceasefire and a nine-month political transition to civilian-led governance.[^cite_note-Eltahir-2025-338][^cite_note-Khaled-2025-339][^cite_note-Abdelaziz-2025-340] However, implementation remained uncertain: the SAF voiced strong reservations, particularly demanding that the RSF withdraw from civilian areas before any truce could take effect.[^cite_note-Eltahir-2025-338][^cite_note-Abdelaziz-2025-340] Furthermore, growing strains between two members of the Quad, Saudi Arabia and the UAE, came to dominate the roadmap negotiations.[^cite_note-341]

Meanwhile, RSF leaders announced that they would accept the Quad's proposal for a "humanitarian ceasefire" to mitigate the civilian toll.[^cite_note-Khaled-2025-339][^cite_note-Abdelaziz-2025-340][^cite_note-342] Some in the SAF posited that the truce would allow the RSF to consolidate gains after the fall of El Fasher.[^cite_note-343][^cite_note-344]

### 2026

#### Government back in Khartoum

In March 2025, the SAF recaptured [Khartoum](17320.md), which had been captured by RSF shortly after the war began in 2023. After being based in [Port Sudan](213572.md) for nearly three years, Sudan's military-led government returned to Khartoum on 11 January 2026, where Prime Minister Kamil Idris said it would start restoring services for a city devastated by war. The city remains badly damaged after mass displacement, looting, occupation of civilian homes, and the near collapse of basic services.[^cite_note-345][^cite_note-346]

#### Kordofan

In early 2026, fighting intensified in the [Kordofan](1127161.md) region, where almost daily drone strikes resulted in substantial civilian casualties and struck markets, health facilities, and residential areas. The shift in the epicentre of the conflict followed the SAF's recapture of Khartoum and the RSF's consolidation of control in Darfur. According to the conflict monitor [ACLED](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ACLED), control of the Kordofan states would strengthen the RSF's ability to regain access to Sudan's central corridor and reinforce its rival administration in western Sudan. Both sides have been accused of carrying out strikes on civilian targets, while hundreds of thousands of people have been [displaced](477535.md) and [famine](51329.md) conditions were reported in parts of [South Kordofan](767653.md).[^cite_note-347]

In late January 2026, the SAF entered Kadugli, the capital of South Kordofan, and broke the nearly two-year RSF siege of Dilling, reopening eastern supply routes into the region even as drone strikes and fighting continued elsewhere in Kordofan.[^cite_note-348]

In late June 2026, the U.S. State Department issued a formal warning that the RSF were massing near the city of [El Obeid](1481380.md). American intelligence sources indicated that “mass atrocities” against civilians may occur if the RSF were to capture El Obeid. In response, the RSF claimed that their activities near El Obeid were nothing more than "just a normal military operation" and that any casualties would be nothing more than "the normal cascade of war".[^cite_note-349]

In late July 2026, the SAF and their allies recaptured the road linking El Obeid and Omdurman. SAF columns moved from the north and south and captured Umm Sayalla, Bara and Jabra al-Sheikh during the initial advance before entering Gereij and Umm Girfa. The road has been controlled by the RSF since the beginning of the civil war and its capture opens one more line of communication for the SAF to supply El Obeid, though RSF fighters might still be located close to the road and may be able to attack army convoys and cut off sections of the road.[^cite_note-350]

#### Darfur

In June 2026, the [Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights](22463.md) released a report on sexual violence in the Darfur Region. According to the Report, sexual violence escalated sharply throughout Darfur throughout 2025 and early 2025. In particular, the UNOHCR noted outbreaks of sexual violence and abductions committed against [Zaghawa](1467480.md) women and girls during RSF offensives, including the 2025 RSF offensives on El Fasher and Zamzam.[^cite_note-351]

## Casualties and war crimes

Further information: [War crimes during the Sudanese civil war (2023–present)](74944426.md) and [casualty recording](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casualty_recording)

The fatality numbers are highly uncertain.[^cite_note-howman-37] According to a report published by *[Le Monde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Monde)* in November 2024, the war may have killed over 150,000 civilians through the combined tolls of bombardments, massacres, starvation and disease.[^cite_note-WS-286] Total deaths could be significantly more than 150,000.[^cite_note-asimgu-38][^cite_note-disbyt-39] A November 2024 report from the [London School of Hygiene & Tropical Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_School_of_Hygiene_&_Tropical_Medicine) estimated more than 61,000 deaths in Khartoum State alone, for the period between April 2023 and June 2024.[^cite_note-352]

Early in the conflict, doctors on the ground warned that reported figures did not include all casualties as people could not reach hospitals due to difficulties in movement.[^cite_note-353] Soon after the war broke out, a spokesperson for the [Sudanese Red Crescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudanese_Red_Crescent) was quoted as saying that the number of casualties "was not small".[^cite_note-Al_Jazeera-119] The [Sultanate of Dar Masalit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masalit_people) claimed on 20 June 2023 that more than 5,000 people were killed and about 8,000 were wounded in fighting in [West Darfur](767645.md) alone,[^cite_note-dabanga5k-354] while a Masalit tribal leader told the Sudanese news outlet [Ayin Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayin_Network) on 22 July 2023 that more than 10,000 people had been killed in the state.[^cite_note-10kcnn-355] Sudanese prosecutors recorded over 500 missing persons cases across the country, some of which were [enforced disappearances](686148.md), and were mostly blamed on the RSF.[^cite_note-356] On 2 May 2024, a US Senate hearing on the war estimated that between 15,000 and 30,000 people had died, but considered that to be an underestimation by a factor of 10 to 15, stating the real death toll could be as high as 150,000.[^cite_note-dabanga150-357] As of 27 May 2024, [Armed Conflict Location and Event Data Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_Conflict_Location_and_Event_Data_Project) data reported 17,044 fatalities.[^cite_note-358]

On 29 March 2025, the Sudanese Group for Defending Rights and Freedoms said that it had recorded 50,000 missing persons cases since the beginning of the war.[^cite_note-359] In early December, authorities reported that thousands of bodies hastily buried by residents and fighters were exhumed from Khartoum and the surrounding area.[^cite_note-Babaee-2025-360] Sources reported that 15,000 bodies were recovered since April 2024.[^cite_note-Babaee-2025-360][^cite_note-Khartoum_state_collects_15-2025-361] Efforts to recover corpses buried outside of [cemeteries](63752.md) began in April 2024 and was estimated to complete recoveries in Khartoum before 2026.[^cite_note-Khartoum_state_collects_15-2025-361]

### Darfur

In [Geneina](1501534.md), West Darfur, ethnic clashes that began in the last week of April 2023 had killed at least 1,100 people,[^cite_note-362] while the [Sultanate of Dar Masalit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masalit_people) claimed that more than 5,000 people were killed and about 8,000 were wounded in the city.[^cite_note-dabanga5k-354] In July 2023, a Masalit tribal leader claimed that more than 10,000 people had been killed in West Darfur alone, and that 80% of Geneina's residents had fled.[^cite_note-10kcnn-355]

Massacres were recorded in towns such as [Tawila](74157994.md)[^cite_note-NK-363] and [Misterei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misterei),[^cite_note-misterei-150] while a mass grave was discovered in Geneina containing the bodies of 87 people killed in clashes.[^cite_note-mass_grave-186] Several intellectuals, politicians, professionals and nobility were assassinated. Most of these atrocities were blamed on the RSF and allied Arab militias. The UK government,[^cite_note-ukcleansing22-364] witnesses and other observers described the violence in the region as tantamount to [ethnic cleansing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing) or even [genocide](12441.md), with non-Arab groups such as the [Masalit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masalit_people) being the primary victims.[^cite_note-NK-363] Mujeebelrahman Yagoub, Assistant Commissioner for Refugees in West Darfur called the violence worse than the [War in Darfur](5043637.md) in 2003 and the [Rwandan genocide](676659.md) in 1994.[^cite_note-365]

### Foreign casualties

| Country | Deaths | Ref. |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [Ethiopia](187749.md) | 15 | [^cite_note-EK-366] |
| [Syria](7515849.md) | 15 | [^cite_note-Al-Awsat_diplo-367] |
| [Democratic Republic of the Congo](76762.md) | 10 | [^cite_note-AA-368] |
| [Eritrea](17238590.md) | 9 | [^cite_note-CC-369] |
| [United States](3434750.md) | 2 | [^cite_note-WH-370] |
| [India](14533.md) | 1 | \[^cite_note-[www.cnn.com-2023-371](http://www.cnn.com-2023-371/)] |
| [Turkey](11125639.md) | 1 | [^cite_note-TT-372] |

Civilians, including 15 Syrians,[^cite_note-Al-Awsat_diplo-367] 15 Ethiopians[^cite_note-EK-366] and nine Eritreans[^cite_note-CC-369] have been killed across the country. An Indian national working in Khartoum died after being hit by a stray bullet on 15 April.\[^cite_note-[www.cnn.com-2023-371](http://www.cnn.com-2023-371/)] Two Americans were killed, including a professor working in the [University of Khartoum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Khartoum) who was stabbed to death while evacuating.[^cite_note-WH-370][^cite_note-373] A two-year-old girl from Turkey was killed while her parents were injured after their house was struck by a rocket on 18 April.[^cite_note-TT-372] Ten students from the [Democratic Republic of the Congo](76762.md) were killed in an SAF airstrike on the [International University of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_University_of_Africa) in Khartoum on 4 June.[^cite_note-AA-368] The SAF claimed that the Egyptian assistant military attaché was killed by RSF fire while driving his car in Khartoum, which was denied by the Egyptian ambassador.[^cite_note-374]
