Two Greek nationals trapped in a church on 15 April sustained leg injuries when caught in crossfire while trying to leave.[^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376] A Filipino migrant worker[^cite_note-377] and an Indonesian student at a school in Khartoum were injured by stray bullets.[^cite_note-378] On 17 April, the [European Union Ambassador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union_Ambassador) to Sudan, [Aidan O'Hara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aidan_O'Hara) of Ireland, was assaulted by unidentified "armed men wearing military fatigues" in his home, he suffered minor injuries and was able to resume working on 19 April.[^cite_note-Ambassador-379][^cite_note-Residents_flee-380] On 23 April, a French evacuation convoy was shot at, injuring one person.[^cite_note-BBC_News-2023-2-381] The French government later confirmed the casualty to be a French soldier.[^cite_note-France538-382] An employee of the Egyptian embassy was shot and injured during an evacuation mission.[^cite_note-383][^cite_note-384]

#### Evacuation of foreign nationals

Main article: [Evacuation of foreign nationals during the Sudanese civil war (2023–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evacuation_of_foreign_nationals_during_the_Sudanese_civil_war_\(2023%E2%80%93present\))



Repatriations through the European Union Civil Protection Mechanism

The outbreak of violence has led foreign governments to monitor the situation in Sudan and move toward the evacuation and repatriation of their nationals. Among some countries with several expatriates in Sudan are [Egypt](8087628.md), which has more than 10,000 citizens in the country,[^cite_note-Foreigners-385] and the United States, which has more than 16,000 citizens, most of whom are [dual nationals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual_national).[^cite_note-386] Efforts at extraction were hampered by the fighting within the capital Khartoum, particularly in and around the airport. This has forced evacuations to be undertaken by road via [Port Sudan](213572.md) on the [Red Sea](26191.md), which lies about 650 km (400 miles) northeast of Khartoum.[^cite_note-387] from where they were airlifted or ferried directly to their home countries or third ones. Other evacuations were undertaken through overland border crossings or airlifts from diplomatic missions and other designated locations with direct involvement of the militaries of some home countries. Some transit hubs used during the evacuation include the port of [Jeddah](277718.md) in Saudi Arabia and [Djibouti](17207794.md), which hosts military bases of the United States, China, Japan, France, and other European countries.[^cite_note-388]

### War crimes

See also: [List of massacres in Sudan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_massacres_in_Sudan)

This section is an excerpt from [War crimes in the Sudanese civil war (2023–present)](74944426.md).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=War_crimes_in_the_Sudanese_civil_war_\(2023%E2%80%93present\)\&action=edit)]

|  | This section needs to be **updated**. The reason given is: **Needs 2026 updates on massacres, drones attacks, shelling, and reaction and investigation to/into the war crimes**. Please help update this article to reflect recent events or newly available information. *(August 2026)* |
| --- | --- |



[Hemedti](60669608.md), commander of the RSF, is sanctioned by the United States government and accused of genocide, war crimes, and sexual violence.

[^cite_note-389]



[Abdel Fattah al-Burhan](60480545.md), commander of the SAF, is sanctioned by the United States government and accused of war crimes and using chemical weapons on civilians.

[^cite_note-390]

In [Geneina](1501534.md), [West Darfur](767645.md), the RSF and [Arab militias](683270.md) killed more than 15,000 non-Arab people.[^cite_note-War_crimes_in_the_Sudanese_civil_war_(2023–present)_\:43-391] On 22 July, a [Masalit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masalit_people) tribal leader claimed that more than 10,000 people had been killed in West Darfur alone, and that 80% of Geneina's residents had fled. [Massacres against the Masalit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Masalit_genocide) were recorded in towns such as [Tawila](74157994.md), [Sirba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sirba_massacre), [Ardamata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ardamata_massacre), [Kutum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kutum_massacre), and [Misterei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misterei_massacre), while a mass grave was discovered around Geneina. The UK[^cite_note-War_crimes_in_the_Sudanese_civil_war_(2023–present)_\:28-392][^cite_note-War_crimes_in_the_Sudanese_civil_war_(2023–present)_ukcleansing22-393] and US[^cite_note-War_crimes_in_the_Sudanese_civil_war_(2023–present)_\:38-394][^cite_note-War_crimes_in_the_Sudanese_civil_war_(2023–present)_\:39-395] governments, witnesses, and other observers described the violence in the region as tantamount to ethnic cleansing[^cite_note-War_crimes_in_the_Sudanese_civil_war_(2023–present)_ukcleansing22-393][^cite_note-War_crimes_in_the_Sudanese_civil_war_(2023–present)_\:35-396] or even genocide,[^cite_note-War_crimes_in_the_Sudanese_civil_war_(2023–present)_\:36-397][^cite_note-War_crimes_in_the_Sudanese_civil_war_(2023–present)_\:37-398][^cite_note-War_crimes_in_the_Sudanese_civil_war_(2023–present)_dabanga5k-399][^cite_note-War_crimes_in_the_Sudanese_civil_war_(2023–present)_\:48-400] with non-Arab groups such as the Masalit being the primary victims. The RSF and Arab militias are also accused of widespread robberies, looting food meant to feed 4.4 million people, and sexual violence against Sudanese and foreign women, particularly Masalit and non-Arab women. NGOs estimate that the actual figure of sexual violence victims could be as high as 4,400.[^cite_note-War_crimes_in_the_Sudanese_civil_war_(2023–present)_auto2-401] In March 2024, UNICEF reported that armed men were raping and sexually assaulting children as young as one year old.[^cite_note-402][^cite_note-403] The UN was urged to start an inquiry, and governments were encouraged to allocate resources to aid survivors.

The RSF and Arab militias in Sudan are also accused of targeted torture and killings of intellectuals, politicians, professionals, and tribal leaders. Notable victims include Adam Zakaria Is'haq, a physician and human rights advocate, and [Khamis Abakar](74121008.md), the governor of West Darfur, who was kidnapped, tortured, and executed.[^cite_note-War_crimes_in_the_Sudanese_civil_war_(2023–present)_\:10-404] The RSF also targeted the families of their opponents, such as [Mustafa Tambour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_Tambour)'s family. The SAF and RSF are accused of threatening, attacking, and killing journalists and activists during the conflict. The [Sudanese Journalists Syndicate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudanese_Journalists_Syndicate) documented over 40 violations in May 2023 alone. Several journalists were injured or killed, and 13 newspapers ceased operations. Humanitarian workers were also targeted, with 18 killed and many others detained.

The [International Criminal Court](14880.md)[^cite_note-War_crimes_in_the_Sudanese_civil_war_(2023–present)_\:5-405][^cite_note-406] and [Amnesty International](18947898.md)[^cite_note-407] are investigating war crimes and crimes against humanity committed during the war. The SAF accused the RSF of perpetrating these crimes. General [Abdel Fattah al-Burhan](60480545.md) (SAF commander) established a committee to investigate these allegations. Several countries proposed a motion to the [UN Human Rights Council](635790.md) for an investigation into the atrocities. The UN Human Rights Council voted to adopt a resolution creating a fact-finding committee on these crimes. Human Rights Watch and the United Nations Integrated Transition Assistance Mission in Sudan have called for measures to protect civilians.

On 11 July 2025, the [International Criminal Court](14880.md) (ICC) reported to the United Nations Security Council that war crimes and crimes against humanity are currently being committed in Sudan's Darfur region, including a severe [humanitarian crisis](73745525.md) with over 30 million people in need, amid the ongoing conflict.

On 25 January 2026, WHO published a report on the situation in terms of public health, stating that around 33 million people are in need of humanitarian assistance, with 21 million more facing a food insecurity[^cite_note-408][^cite_note-409].

### Accusations of chemical warfare

During the war, multiple reports have alleged the use of chemical weapons by the SAF. France 24’s Observers unit, using open‑source imagery and witness testimony, concluded that SAF aircraft dropped barrels of chlorine gas near the al‑Jaili refinery in 2024.[^cite_note-410] In 2025 the United States government formally determined that Sudan had used chemical weapons in the conflict,[^cite_note-411] and in 2026 it stated at the Organisation for the Prohibition of Chemical Weapons (OPCW) that Sudan remained in non‑compliance with the Chemical Weapons Convention, announcing sanctions and calling for immediate international inspections of suspected chemical‑weapons sites.[^cite_note-412] In July 2026, Urwa al-Sadiq, the assistant head of the National Umma Party, accused the SAF-aligned authorities under General al-Burhan of implementing a policy of denial and "procedural maneuvering" to delay inspections, while systematically cordoning off and cleaning up affected zones—including Khartoum Hospital, the [Sudan National Broadcasting Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudan_National_Broadcasting_Corporation) facilities, and the al-Jaili refinery—to conceal physical evidence of chemical weapons usage.[^cite_note-413]

## Foreign involvement

Main article: [Foreign involvement in Sudanese civil war (2023–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_involvement_in_Sudanese_civil_war_\(2023%E2%80%93present\))



&#x20; Sudan

&#x20; Countries supplying arms to the SAF

&#x20; Countries supplying arms to the RSF

As the conflict has evolved, it has increasingly taken on regional dimensions. Analysts describe the war as shaped not only by internal rivalries but also by the involvement of external actors providing financial, military, and diplomatic support to the warring parties, contributing to dynamics resembling a [proxy war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proxy_war).[^cite_note-3scenarios-414]

### Foreign arms supplies to both sides

In June 2024, a briefing by [Amnesty International](18947898.md) stated that the constant flow of foreign weapons is fueling the war and breaching the Darfur arms embargo. The organization found that the recently manufactured or transferred weapons and ammunition were being imported in large quantities into Sudan from China, Russia, Turkey, Yemen, the UAE and Serbia. The weapons supply has impacted the war by causing massive civilian displacement and a humanitarian crisis in Sudan. Both warring sides were using Chinese-manufactured advanced drone jammers, mortars and anti-material rifles. The RSF were also reported to be using recently manufactured armoured personnel carriers from the UAE.[^cite_note-FR-415][^cite_note-416] In October 2025, the SAF recovered boxes of arms, ammunitions and medicines supplied by the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) from an area previously held by the RSF in southeast Sudan.[^cite_note-Sudan_Army_finds_UAE_arms_supplied_to_RSF_rebels-2024-417] The UAE had been previously known for their support to the Sudanese military, and marginalised civilian rule by promoting the idea of Hemedti to helm the country's economic policy "in the interests of a stable transition".[^cite_note-418] The RSF primarily funds its operations through gold exports to supporting countries, including the UAE, Kenya and Ethiopia. In 2025, after declaring their support for the RSF, Ethiopia and Kenya published quarterly gold production figures that were double those of the previous year.[^cite_note-Julien-Thomas-2026-54]

### Canada

A federal department referred a contract from Canadian lobbying firm Dickens & Madsen Inc. to the RCMP for investigation after Amnesty International asked the government of Canada to investigate this over possible violations on sanctions in Sudan. The contract was signed between Hemedti and [Ari Ben-Menashe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ari_Ben-Menashe) (head of the company) in May 2019 for US\$6 million, a month before the Khartoum massacre.[^cite_note-419] The aim was to present the military regime in positive light, securing meetings with potential oil investors and officials from US, Libya, Saudi Arabia and Russia to strengthen international ties, funding and equipment.[^cite_note-420]

In November 2025, Mark Carney visited to [Abu Dhabi](18950756.md) to meet with the UAE president. Carney said he discussed the Sudan civil war during this meeting, though the details are unclear.[^cite_note-421] In addition, Canada exports weapons to the UAE; however, the UAE insists these weapons do not flow into the hands of the RSF.[^cite_note-422]

Despite this, Canadian weapons have been seen used by RSF soldiers. In 2016, a United Nations panel accused Canadian company [STREIT Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STREIT_Group) of breaking the arms embargo against Sudan. The allegation involved a 2012 sales of 24 armoured vehicles. This is the third time the UN has condemned the company's actions, which violated the terms of the UN [Arms Trade Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arms_Trade_Treaty), signed by Canada in 2019 to prohibit the export of arms to Sudan directly or through third countries. STREIT Group claimed that the exports do not violate controls because they do not have weapons attached to them.[^cite_note-423][^cite_note-424][^cite_note-York-2025-425]

There has also been documentation of STREIT Group's armored vehicles over the years. RSF soldiers were also seen posting on social media over the years in armored vehicles manufactured by the group, along with rifles manufactured by another Canadian company, [Sterling Cross Defense Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sterling_Cross_Defense_Systems).[^cite_note-York-2025-425][^cite_note-426][^cite_note-427]

### Chad

See also: [Chad–Sudan relations](16010780.md)

On 7 June 2023, Hissein Alamine Tchaw-tchaw, a Chadian dissident who belongs to the same ethnic group as Hemedti and claims to be the leader of the Movement for the Fight of the Oppressed in Chad (MFOC), which is fighting the government of President [Mahamat Déby](39073394.md), posted a video showing his participation in an RSF attack on the Yarmouk munitions factory in Khartoum.[^cite_note-428]

On 17 November 2023, the SLM-Minnawi and the [Justice and Equality Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_and_Equality_Movement) (JEM) accused the Chadian government of supporting the RSF, and "supplying it with military equipment and mercenaries by opening its territory and airspace".[^cite_note-429] A report from *Africa Analyst* alleged that Chadian soldiers belonging to a joint Chadian-Sudanese command under [Osman Bahr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osman_Bahr?action=edit\&redlink=1) intercepted a shipment of military equipment intended for the RSF on its way from [N'Djamena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N'Djamena) and gave it instead to the JEM, which the latter denied.[^cite_note-430][^cite_note-431] *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* linked Chad's junta receiving financial support from the UAE in exchange for allowing it to support the RSF through Amdjarass airport.[^cite_note-Walsh-432][^cite_note-eco-433]

Following accusations by SAF deputy commander Yasser al-Atta of Chadian government support for the RSF, the Chadian government unsuccessfully demanded an apology from the Sudanese ambassador and expelled four Sudanese diplomats from the country on 17 December.[^cite_note-434]

On 5 November 2024, the government of Sudan filed a complaint with the [African Commission on Human and Peoples' Rights](1620260.md) (ACHPR) demanding reparations from Chad for their support of the RSF, accusing Chad of violating international law.[^cite_note-435]

On 23 February 2026, Chad shut its eastern border with Sudan indefinitely, citing repeated incursions by Sudanese armed groups and growing insecurity near its territory.[^cite_note-chashu-436][^cite_note-437] The decision followed heavy fighting in the border town of al-Tina between the RSF and forces aligned with the Sudanese army, and Chad said limited humanitarian exemptions could still be granted with prior approval.[^cite_note-chashu-436]

### Egypt

See also: [Egypt–Sudan relations](22334497.md)

On 15 April 2023, RSF forces claimed, via Twitter, to have taken Egyptian troops prisoner near [Merowe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merowe,_Sudan),[^cite_note-438][^cite_note-439] and a military plane carrying markings of the [Egyptian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Air_Force) (EAF).[^cite_note-AJreturn-53] Initially, no official explanation was given for the Egyptian soldiers' presence, while Egypt and Sudan have had military cooperation due to diplomatic tensions with [Ethiopia](187749.md).[^cite_note-440] Later on, the [Egyptian Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Armed_Forces) stated that around 200 of its soldiers were in Sudan to conduct exercises with the Sudanese military.[^cite_note-BBC_News-112] Around that time, the SAF reportedly encircled RSF forces in Merowe airbase. As a result, the Egyptian Armed Forces announced that it was following the situation as a precaution for the safety of its personnel.[^cite_note-Al_Jazeera-119] The RSF later stated that it would cooperate in repatriating the soldiers to Egypt.[^cite_note-AJreturn-53] On 19 April, the RSF stated that it had moved the soldiers to Khartoum and would hand them over when the "appropriate opportunity" arose.[^cite_note-441] Of the captured Egyptian troops, 177 were released and flown back to Egypt aboard three Egyptian military planes that took off from Khartoum airport later in the day. The remaining 27 soldiers, who were from the Egyptian Air Force, were sheltered at the Egyptian embassy and later evacuated.[^cite_note-442][^cite_note-443]

On 16 April 2023, the RSF claimed that its troops in Port Sudan were attacked by foreign aircraft and issued a warning against any foreign interference.[^cite_note-egy1-444] According to former [CIA](5183633.md) analyst Cameron Hudson, Egyptian fighter jets were a part of these bombing campaigns against the RSF, and Egyptian special forces units have been deployed and are providing intelligence and tactical support to the SAF.[^cite_note-egy2-445] *The Wall Street Journal* said that Egypt had sent fighter jets and pilots to support the Sudanese military.[^cite_note-for1-446] On 17 April, satellite imagery obtained by *The War Zone* revealed that one Egyptian Air Force [MiG-29M2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikoyan_MiG-29M#Variants) fighter jet had been destroyed and two others had been damaged or destroyed while stationed at [Merowe Airbase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merowe_Airport). A Sudanese Air Force [Guizhou JL-9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guizhou_JL-9) was among the destroyed aircraft.[^cite_note-447] After initial confusion, the RSF accepted the explanation that Egyptian combat and support personnel were conducting exercises with the Sudanese military before the outbreak of hostilities.[^cite_note-BBC_News-112]

Egypt's position is driven primarily by national security concerns, including stability along its southern border, Nile water security, and the regional balance of power. Cairo has long viewed the SAF as its most reliable institutional partner, reflecting decades of cooperation on border security and counterterrorism. As the RSF expanded its capabilities during the war, Egypt increasingly perceived it as a destabilising force, particularly if it advanced toward northern Sudan. These concerns, heightened by tensions with Ethiopia over the [Grand Ethiopian Renaissance Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Ethiopian_Renaissance_Dam) (GERD), have reinforced Egypt's preference for a unified Sudanese state and its opposition to fragmentation.[^cite_note-3scenarios-414]

Since at least mid-2025, drones launched from an air base in Egypt’s [Western Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Desert), along the border with Sudan, have supported the SAF by striking RSF targets. This marked Egypt’s direct entry into the war; before this, support for the Sudanese government was primarily diplomatic.[^cite_note-EgyptSudanDrones-22]

### Ethiopia

See also: [Ethiopia–Sudan relations](46667065.md) and [Al-Fashaga conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Fashaga_conflict)

[Ethiopia](187749.md) initially supported the RSF, which was viewed by Addis Ababa as a useful counterweight to both Egyptian influence in Sudan and the SAF, while some reports also described the RSF as having assisted Ethiopia against the [Tigray People's Liberation Front](1276759.md) during the [Tigray War](65760352.md).[^cite_note-CS-448][^cite_note-sudanethiopia-449] Relations between Addis Ababa and Khartoum deteriorated sharply after the outbreak of the Sudanese civil war in April 2023, as the government of [Abiy Ahmed](56624029.md) was increasingly accused by Sudanese officials of indirectly supporting the RSF through cooperation with the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) (UAE). Hemedti visited Ethiopia in December 2023 in an attempt to push for negotiations with the SAF, further fueling speculation regarding Ethiopian ties with the paramilitary group.[^cite_note-450]

In July 2024, Prime Minister Abiy Ahmed traveled to [Port Sudan](213572.md) for talks with General al-Burhan, in what observers described as a possible shift in Ethiopia’s position on the conflict.[^cite_note-sudanethiopia-449][^cite_note-451] Despite the diplomatic outreach, tensions along the Ethiopia–Sudan border continued to rise. On 4 July 2025, senior Sudanese officials accused Ethiopia of exploiting the civil war by deploying army-backed militias into the disputed [Al-Fashaga District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Fashaga_District), where militias allegedly blocked Sudanese farmers and cleared agricultural land under the protection of the [Ethiopian National Defense Force](9405.md). Sudanese officials further alleged that Ethiopian armed groups crossed into the border district of [Al Galabat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Galabat_District) to loot livestock markets amid a security vacuum created by the redeployment of Sudanese troops to the civil war frontlines.[^cite_note-452]

In February 2026, *[Reuters](18998750.md)* reported that Ethiopia was hosting a secret camp in the Benishangul-Gumuz region to train thousands of RSF fighters near the Sudanese border, describing the facility as “the first direct evidence of Ethiopia's involvement” in the war. According to Reuters, eight sources, including a senior Ethiopian government official, alleged that the UAE financed the camp’s construction and supplied military trainers, logistics, and drone infrastructure linked to nearby Asosa airport, while an internal Ethiopian security note reportedly stated that 4,300 fighters were training at the site in January 2026. Reuters and regional analysts also alleged that the airport had become instrumental in supplying the RSF across the border into Sudan.[^cite_note-EthiopiaBase-453]

In May 2026, Sudan formally accused Ethiopia and the UAE of orchestrating drone attacks on [Khartoum International Airport](4306874.md) and military installations in the capital, describing the strikes as “direct aggression” against Sudanese sovereignty.[^cite_note-BBCEthiopia-454] General al-Burhan alleged that drones used in the attack originated from Bahir Dar airport in Ethiopia and accused the government of Abiy Ahmed of facilitating military assistance to the [Rapid Support Forces](43053874.md), including drone support aimed at Khartoum airport.[^cite_note-EthiopiaSudanWar-455] Ethiopia rejected the accusations as “baseless,” while the UAE denied involvement and dismissed the allegations as propaganda.[^cite_note-BBCEthiopia-454]

The accusations deepened fears that the Sudanese civil war had evolved into a wider regional proxy conflict involving Ethiopia, the UAE, [Egypt](8087628.md), and [Eritrea](17238590.md), with Addis Ababa allegedly viewing the RSF as a counterweight to the SAF’s alignment with Cairo and Asmara. Investigations by Reuters, the BBC, *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)*, and regional analysts linked the UAE to extensive financial and military backing for the RSF, including alleged arms transfers, logistics networks, and support infrastructure operating through Ethiopian territory.[^cite_note-EthiopiaBase-453][^cite_note-456][^cite_note-BBCEthiopia-454][^cite_note-EthiopiaSudanWar-455]

### Libyan National Army

See also: [Libya–Sudan relations](5625217.md)

The Egypt-backed [Libyan National Army](35457665.md), under the command of [Khalifa Haftar](31227894.md), dispatched aircraft to fly military supplies to the RSF before the outbreak of hostilities.[^cite_note-for1-446][^cite_note-GuardianHaftar-457] Haftar and the LNA collaborated with the [Wagner Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wagner_Group), a Russian [private military company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_military_company), to conduct these flights.[^cite_note-GuardianHaftar-457] Sudan has also accused the LNA of launching joint border attacks with the RSF.[^cite_note-458] Khalifa Haftar has reportedly denied these claims.[^cite_note-libya-ahram-459]

Haftar's support for a different faction in Sudan than the Egyptian government was commented on by *[The New Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Arab)*, which viewed it as a sign of Egyptian weakness due to economic malaise and reliance on Haftar to police Eastern Libya, which constitutes a security concern for the Egyptian government. *The New Arab* also viewed the LNA's role in the conflict as signifying a shift in its diplomatic orientation, from being primarily backed by Egypt to being primarily backed by the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) (UAE).[^cite_note-460]

### Russia

See also: [Russia–Sudan relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia%E2%80%93Sudan_relations)

For much of the Sudanese civil war Russia has sent weapons to both the RSF and SAF. This began to shift during mid-2024, with the Russian government beginning to favour the SAF, concurrent with Russia–SAF discussions around the construction of a Russian naval base north of Port Sudan.[^cite_note-MEERussia-49][^cite_note-461] The same year, Russia began delivering large quantities of weapons, jet components, fuel, and drones to the Sudanese government in its effort against the RSF, allowing the SAF to recapture parts of the capital, Khartoum, from the RSF.[^cite_note-462]

Russia's involvement is also driven by broader strategic and economic interests, including efforts to secure a naval presence on the Red Sea and access to Sudanese gold resources. These resources have been particularly valuable in mitigating the impact of Western sanctions. In addition to its military engagement, Russia has used its diplomatic position, including at the United Nations, to protect its interests and resist ceasefire initiatives.[^cite_note-3scenarios-414]

#### Wagner Group

See also: [Wagner Group activities in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wagner_Group_activities_in_Africa)

According to [CNN](62028.md), Wagner supplied [surface-to-air missiles](182664.md) to the RSF, picking up the items from [Syria](7515849.md) and delivering some of them by plane to Haftar-controlled bases in Libya to be then delivered to the RSF, while dropping other items directly to RSF positions in northwestern Sudan.[^cite_note-463] American officials said that Wagner was offering to supply additional weapons to the RSF from its existing stocks in the [Central African Republic](5478.md) (CAR).[^cite_note-NYTCAR-56] On 6 September 2023, Wagner reportedly deployed a convoy of more than 100 vehicles carrying weapons to the RSF garrison in [al-Zurug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Zurug?action=edit\&redlink=1) from Chad.[^cite_note-Ukraine-23] SAF Lieutenant General [Yasser al-Atta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasser_al-Atta) also accused the Wagner Group of bringing in mercenaries from several African nations to fight alongside the RSF.[^cite_note-464][^cite_note-atta-465] The head of the Wagner Group, [Yevgeny Prigozhin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yevgeny_Prigozhin),[^cite_note-466] and the RSF denied the allegations.[^cite_note-libya-ahram-459]

As relations between the Russian government and the SAF improved during mid-2024, the latter publicly claimed that the Wagner Group was no longer operating in Sudan.[^cite_note-467] This claim was contradicted by a diplomatic source and eyewitnesses speaking to *[Middle East Eye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_East_Eye)* (*MEE*).[^cite_note-MEERussia-49]

### Saudi Arabia

See also: [Saudi Arabia–Sudan relations](56310352.md)

Saudi Arabia has provided military support and financial aid to the SAF, although it officially denies doing so,[^cite_note-468] in an effort to counterbalance the influence of the UAE, which supports the RSF.[^cite_note-saudiuaesudan-51] Several analyses have consequently described the conflict as a proxy war between the two Gulf states.[^cite_note-saudiuaesudan-51][^cite_note-469][^cite_note-470]

Saudi Arabia's engagement is also shaped by security concerns related to the [Red Sea crisis](75207766.md) and a preference for a unified Sudanese state capable of containing armed groups and preventing regional spillover. Saudi policy has remained broadly aligned with the SAF and coordinated with Egypt, reflecting shared concerns over fragmentation and border instability. Its support has largely taken diplomatic and political forms, avoiding direct military escalation.[^cite_note-3scenarios-414]

In March 2025, al-Burhan visited Saudi Arabia in his first trip outside Sudan since [the SAF retook Khartoum](73563331.md). There, he thanked Saudi support for Sudanese unity and the fight against the RSF.[^cite_note-471]

### South Sudan

See also: [South Sudan–Sudan relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Sudan%E2%80%93Sudan_relations)

Since the outbreak of renewed violence in Sudan in 2023, South Sudan has adopted a mediatory role, urging peace and engaging with IGAD and the AU, though with limited success due to the conflict's complexity and multiple factions. South Sudan is deeply concerned about spillover effects—such as refugee flows and economic instability—and recognizes that its own fragile stability is tied to Sudan's fate.[^cite_note-472][^cite_note-473] Tensions escalated further with a February 2025 alliance between Sudan's RSF and the [SPLM–N](30884032.md), a rebel group near the South Sudanese border. Experts warn this could pull South Sudan into the conflict, especially if the Sudanese Army supports rival South Sudanese militias in response. With shared borders, historical ties, and existing political tensions between South Sudan's leaders (President [Salva Kiir Mayardit](2363203.md) and Vice President [Riek Machar](2407248.md)), the risk of both wars merging is high. The strategic location of the RSF-SPLM–N alliance also boosts smuggling and military operations, weakening the Sudanese Army and increasing regional instability. If left unchecked, experts fear the two conflicts could become indistinguishable, worsening humanitarian crises in both countries.[^cite_note-474][^cite_note-475]

### Turkey

See also: [Sudan–Turkey relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudan%E2%80%93Turkey_relations)

Turkey's engagement reflects a combination of strategic ambition and commercial interests in Sudan and the wider Red Sea region. In addition to its role as a security partner, Ankara has sought to expand its economic footprint, including potential access to mineral resources and port infrastructure. This dual approach, combining defence cooperation with diplomatic outreach, has allowed Turkey to increase its influence while maintaining flexibility in its relations with different actors.[^cite_note-3scenarios-414]

Turkey appears to be engaging with both sides, notably through [Baykar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baykar), owned by President [Recep Tayyip Erdoğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan)'s son-in-law, selling \$120 million worth of weapons, 6 TB2 [UCAV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UCAV)'s, 3 ground control stations, 600 warheads to the SAF in 2023, violating US and EU sanctions.[^cite_note-FDDTurkey-50][^cite_note-476] Meanwhile, Arca Defense, another Turkish company, had extensive contact with RSF's procurement officer, though it denies selling weapons, adding complexity to Turkey's role.[^cite_note-477] Turkey's interests include expanding military and diplomatic ties in the Horn of Africa, offering to mediate between Sudan and the UAE in December 2024.[^cite_note-478]

In January 2025, the Somali government agreed to host SAF troops at [Camp TURKSOM](55425081.md) for training, as part of a Turkish-led effort to bolster military support to the SAF.[^cite_note-479][^cite_note-480]

### United Arab Emirates

See also: [Sudan–United Arab Emirates relations](79424486.md) and [United Arab Emirates war crimes § Sudan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_Emirates_war_crimes#Sudan)

The UAE has faced mounting accusations of providing military support to RSF,[^cite_note-atta-465][^cite_note-481][^cite_note-SkynewsUAE-55] including covert arms transfers, drone supply,[^cite_note-482] and logistics routed through Chad, Libya, CAR,[^cite_note-483] and South Sudan.[^cite_note-484][^cite_note-485][^cite_note-Sudan_Army_finds_UAE_arms_supplied_to_RSF_rebels-2024-417] Reports by major outlets like the *Wall Street Journal* (*WSJ*),[^cite_note-486] *New York Times* (*NYT*),[^cite_note-Walsh-432] *The Guardian*,[^cite_note-Smoking_Gun-487] and BBC,[^cite_note-iran_drones-52] along with diplomatic sources and satellite evidence, suggest Emirati cargo planes delivered weapons disguised as aid, with operations coordinated through [Amdjarass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amdjarass_\(city\)) airport in Chad.[^cite_note-Smoking_Gun-487][^cite_note-488] UAE denied the accusations.[^cite_note-489][^cite_note-MEECAR-490][^cite_note-491]

Sudan expelled Emirati diplomats,[^cite_note-492] accused the UAE at the UN of aiding genocide,[^cite_note-493][^cite_note-494][^cite_note-495] and submitted complaints to the [International Criminal Court](14880.md) and the [International Court of Justice](14918.md) (ICJ).[^cite_note-496][^cite_note-497] The residence of the UAE ambassador to Sudan in Khartoum was also attacked on 29 September 2024.[^cite_note-498] The UAE was accused of using humanitarian cover such as Red Crescent hospitals for military purposes, including drone operations and weapon bunkers near the border.[^cite_note-499][^cite_note-500][^cite_note-501] Sudan claimed these actions aimed to maintain Emirati influence and gold interests in Sudan, backed by historical investments and ongoing port and agriculture projects.[^cite_note-TG-24-05-2024-502][^cite_note-503][^cite_note-504]

The UAE's ties to the RSF date back to the [Yemeni civil war](46215853.md) in 2018. Its involvement is said to include cooperation with the Wagner Group for arms deliveries and financing RSF logistics from within the Emirates.[^cite_note-505][^cite_note-506] Identity documents recovered from a 2024 plane crash in Sudan included a [Russian passport](8887086.md) and an ID that linked to a UAE-based company.[^cite_note-507][^cite_note-508]

The US and the UK have called on the UAE to halt support,[^cite_note-509][^cite_note-510] with US lawmakers introducing multiple bills to block arms sales to Abu Dhabi.[^cite_note-511][^cite_note-512] The EU[^cite_note-513][^cite_note-514][^cite_note-515] and Human Rights Watch[^cite_note-516] also demanded accountability. Emirati diplomatic initiatives toward Sudan continued, such as hosting a humanitarian conference and pledging \$200 million aid—actions seen by Sudan as attempts by UAE to improve its image.[^cite_note-517]

On 30 April 2025 UAE authorities said they had intercepted millions of rounds of ammunition at an airport in the UAE which was being illegally transferred to the SAF, which the latter denied.[^cite_note-518]

Sudan opened a case at the International Court of Justice (ICJ) accusing the UAE of breaching the [Genocide Convention](143988.md) by arming and funding the Rapid Support Forces. The court hearings began on 10 April 2025.[^cite_note-519] On 5 May, the court dismissed the case, stating it "manifestly lacks" the authority to continue the proceedings, as the UAE holds a carveout to the provision of the Genocide Convention that grants the court jurisdiction over such cases.[^cite_note-520]

Since the beginning of the Sudanese war, the UAE has been using the [Bosaso International Airport Co](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosaso_Airport) (BIAC) as a key logistical hub to supply the RSF with arms and mercenaries. Due to its strategic location and the UAE's close ties with [Puntland](233569.md)'s leadership, Bosaso Airport serves as a crucial transit point for Emirati weapons and Colombian paramilitaries affiliated with Abu Dhabi's Global Security Service Group (GSSG) to Sudan. In September 2025, Sudan urged Somalia to cease the operations taking place in [Bosaso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosaso).[^cite_note-521][^cite_note-522][^cite_note-523]

Sudan's Foreign Ministry accused the UAE of making "desperate efforts" at the [Non-Aligned Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement) meetings to protect the RSF from condemnation and undercut international solidarity with Sudan. The Ministry said Abu Dhabi should not be allowed to exploit global forums, citing its suggestion of an alternative government.[^cite_note-524][^cite_note-525]

In August 2025, the Sudanese government released a statement accusing regional and international communities of targeting Sudan and supporting the RSF's aggression. It further claimed that the presence of numerous foreign mercenaries posed a significant threat to the nation's peace and security. The government asserted that it possessed undeniable evidence showing that UAE authorities had sponsored and financed mercenaries from [Colombia](5222.md) and other neighboring countries.[^cite_note-Farmer-2025-526][^cite_note-527]

The UAE is the primary purchaser of Sudanese gold from RSF-controlled areas.[^cite_note-Julien-Thomas-2026-54] Through UAE's economic networks gold is channeled from Darfur and other conflict zones into global markets, generating liquidity and linking the RSF to transnational brokerage networks. Flexible, informal channels help move supplies, personnel and capital, sustaining the RSF's military capacity and its political relevance.[^cite_note-528]

### United States

See also: [Sudan–United States relations](15996801.md)

On 20 January 2025, the [Trump administration](78298108.md) froze [USAID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Agency_for_International_Development) payments for 90 days, redirecting most funds to military aid.[^cite_note-AF-529][^cite_note-US-530] This resulted in the closure of hundreds of soup kitchens, and increased deaths from starvation.[^cite_note-531] A court ordered the freeze lifted on 13 February, but the administration cancelled nearly 10,000 aid contracts instead. The judge later demanded payments by 26 February, but Chief Justice [John G. Roberts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_G._Roberts) paused the order pending a [Supreme Court](31737.md) ruling by 28 February.[^cite_note-532]

The US announced a diplomatic meeting of the International Quartet on Sudan, aiming to develop a unified vision to end the war, stop foreign involvement and secure a ceasefire. Scheduled for 29 July 2025 in Washington D.C., the meeting was to include the United States, Saudi Arabia, the UAE and Egypt.[^cite_note-533][^cite_note-534] However, it was postponed at the last minute.[^cite_note-535] Meanwhile, a coalition of Sudanese political parties rejected the UAE as a mediator, calling it "morally unqualified" due to its backing of the RSF.[^cite_note-536]

A 2026 *Reuters* investigation found that US [Special Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Army_Special_Forces) veteran and government contractor Steven Shaulis had operated at least three planes servicing "key" RSF logistics hubs—two operating since at least October 2024—possibly in violation of international sanctions. The planes flew along supply lines used by the RSF to carry out the [siege](73754779.md) and [capture of El Fasher](81461601.md).[^cite_note-537]

## Humanitarian impact

Main article: [Humanitarian impact of the Sudanese civil war (2023–present)](73745525.md)

The war has triggered a severe [humanitarian crisis](1251700.md). Within days into the fighting, communities faced severe shortages of food, water, medical supplies and fuel, with Khartoum and its surroundings hit hardest.[^cite_note-538] In the first months of the war, about 25 million people out of a population of roughly 50 million, required humanitarian assistance.[^cite_note-539] Aid delivery was hampered as supplies were looted.[^cite_note-540][^cite_note-541] By September 2024, roughly 80% of healthcare facilities in Sudan were no longer functional.[^cite_note-criins-542] In October 2025, the [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO) relayed reports that over 450 people had been massacred in El Fasher's last functioning hospital.[^cite_note-l607-543] WHO director general [Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus](39712205.md) declared in 2026 that 2,036 people had been killed in 231 healthcare-related attacks.[^cite_note-l607-543]

### Displacement

The conflict has [forcibly displaced](477535.md) nearly [12 million people inside and outside Sudan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudanese_refugee_crisis_\(2023%E2%80%93present\)),[^cite_note-Operational_data_portal-544] making it one of the largest displacement crises in recent history.[^cite_note-UN-2025-47] By April 2025, the [famine in Sudan](77186018.md) had severely affected nearly 25 million people,[^cite_note-SF-65] including nearly 4 million acutely malnourished children under the age of five.[^cite_note-sudchi-42] By September 2025, the number of people requiring humanitarian aid had increased to 30.4 million.[^cite_note-sidasept-545] Fatality figures remained highly uncertain,[^cite_note-howman-37] with some assessments suggesting the true number may exceed 150,000.[^cite_note-asimgu-38][^cite_note-disbyt-39]

## Analysis

### 2024

[Kholood Khair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kholood_Khair?action=edit\&redlink=1), director of policy organization Confluence Advisory,[^cite_note-546] analyzed the conflict as unfolding simultaneously at a local, national, and regional level.

- At the local level, she stated that much of the violence is shaped by factors specific to individual communities and unresolved grievances predating the current war, resulting in localized and often retaliatory forms of violence. [Alex de Waal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alex_de_Waal) added that the combatants displayed an extreme brutality, arguing that the conduct of fighters reflected not only a sense of [impunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impunity), but also had devastating consequences for Sudanese society.

  [^cite_note-inswor-320]

- At the national level, Khair described the conflict primarily as a struggle between the Sudanese Armed Forces and the Rapid Support Forces, alongside a range of militias and civilian actors aligned with either side. This dimension has attracted the greatest international attention.

  [^cite_note-inswor-320]

- Khair further identified an international dimension, in which Sudan had become entangled in broader geopolitical rivalries. She described the conflict as intersecting with competition between regional groupings, including states aligned with Saudi Arabia, Qatar, Turkey, and Egypt on one side, and a bloc associated with the United Arab Emirates and Israel on the other. However, according to Khair, Sudan had not become a priority issue in high-level international diplomacy, and was rarely discussed.

  [^cite_note-inswor-320]

### 2026

A 2026 policy analysis identifies three main scenarios for the trajectory of the war: a continued stalemate leading to de facto territorial fragmentation, a negotiated settlement driven by shifts in external support, or a decisive military breakthrough followed by renewed instability and reprisals.[^cite_note-547] The analysis argues that the relative likelihood of each scenario depends on the interaction between internal fragmentation and the strategic calculations of external actors.

## Economy

See also: [Economy of Sudan §  During the civil war (2023–present)](27426.md#_During_the_civil_war_\(2023%E2%80%93present\))

### 2023–2024

Sudan's economy was seriously damaged by the conflict, with a near standstill in formal economic activity, particularly in Khartoum and parts of Darfur.[^cite_note-f24economyfeb2024-548][^cite_note-80m-549]

The economy contracted heavily in 2023 and was expected to shrink further in 2024, while state revenues declined steeply.[^cite_note-550] Currency depreciation accelerated,[^cite_note-bilofe-551] international trade dropped,[^cite_note-f24economyfeb2024-548] and gold production fell sharply[^cite_note-552] amid allegations of large scale looting.[^cite_note-bilofe-551] The fighting also rendered more than 60% of Sudan's agricultural land out of service.[^cite_note-f24economyfeb2024-548] By 2024, both warring parties were reported to be financing their operations partly through the sale of [gum arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gum_arabic).[^cite_note-553]

## Disinformation

Throughout the Sudan conflict, the RSF have waged disinformation campaigns, using social media to manipulate public opinion, spread narratives and deny the massacres that are still happening.[^cite_note-Suleiman-2023-554][^cite_note-555] The RSF ran digital propaganda teams from Khartoum and Dubai, using verified social media accounts to distribute misleading content. The RSF were verified on Twitter and has launched a disinformation campaign against the SAF, accusing them of attacking civilians.[^cite_note-Suleiman-2023-554][^cite_note-556] The SAF used [Twitter](9988187.md) for morale-boosting and to counter RSF claims, though some posts were proven false.[^cite_note-557][^cite_note-558]

Widespread disinformation included recycled footage from video games, past conflicts like Ukraine and Libya, and even archaeological props misrepresented as war crimes.[^cite_note-559] For instance, the SAF posted a video allegedly showing recent air operations, which was actually from the video game [Arma 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arma_3).[^cite_note-Al_Jazeera-2-560] The SAF also circulated altered images, including a fabricated photo of Hemedti hospitalized in [Nairobi](21482.md).[^cite_note-561][^cite_note-juhainah-562]

The RSF shared footage of an alleged SAF and [Egyptian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian_Air_Force) (EAF) warplane reportedly shot down by the RSF found to be that of an [Su-25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Su-25) fighter jet that crashed in [Mali](19127.md),[^cite_note-563] and the other of a Libyan aircraft taken outside Sudan in 2020.[^cite_note-564]

The RSF also sent bulletins to UK politicians with the help of Dubai-based Capital Tap Holdings, aiming to counter what it called "disproportionate" disinformation.[^cite_note-PE-565] Facebook removed RSF pages in August 2024, citing policy violations. The RSF blamed the SAF for instigating the ban and said it was negotiating with Meta to restore its accounts.[^cite_note-sudantribune.com-566]

The conflict's information space has been further destabilized by false claims against organizations like the [Sudanese Doctors Syndicate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudanese_Doctors_Syndicate) (SDU)[^cite_note-567] and by [deepfake](56641018.md)-like imagery. Disinformation experts, including Kyle Walter of [Logically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logically_\(company\)), warned that [generative AI](73291755.md) may be fueling the sophistication of fake content, undermining trust in all sources of information.[^cite_note-PE-565]

### El Fasher

Further information: [El Fasher massacre § Reactions and response](81461601.md#Reactions_and_response)

After El Fasher was captured by the RSF, according to *Middle East Eye*, Emirati, Israeli, and far-right influencers tried to falsely frame the conflict as a sectarian one where [Islamists](15012.md) were committing genocide against [Christians](18337522.md).[^cite_note-568]

## Sanctions

### United States

U.S. president [Joe Biden](145422.md) issued an executive order on 4 May 2023 authorizing sanctions against actors destabilizing the country.[^cite_note-569] The first sanctions followed in June, targeting companies linked to both the SAF and RSF, along with visa restrictions on unnamed individuals.[^cite_note-570] Subsequent rounds of sanctions included RSF leaders [Abdul Rahim Dagalo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Rahim_Dagalo) and [Abdel Rahman Jumma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdel_Rahman_Jumma) (accused of killing West Darfur's governor), Islamist leader [Ali Karti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Ahmed_Karti),[^cite_note-571] firms in Sudan and Russia, and former Bashir regime officials involved in RSF support or coup plots.[^cite_note-572] In May 2024, more RSF commanders were sanctioned for violence in North and Central Darfur.[^cite_note-573][^cite_note-574] On 7 January 2025, the U.S. said it had determined that the RSF and allied militias committed genocide in Sudan and imposed [sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctions_\(law\)) on RSF leader Hemedti and affiliated entities to hold them accountable for systematic atrocities and reaffirmed support for Sudanese civil society and a peaceful, democratic future. However, critics said the measures came too late and would have limited impact.[^cite_note-575][^cite_note-576] On 22 May 2025, the U.S. announced new sanctions on Sudan over the SAF's use of chemical weapons against the RSF.[^cite_note-577]

### United Arab Emirates

One affiliated entity that received sanctions was a UAE LLC; according to *[Watan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watan_\(newspaper\))*, in response, the UAE began lobbying in Washington to avoid direct sanctions.[^cite_note-578] The UAE launched an investigation into the entities and reported that none of these seven companies hold a valid commercial license in the UAE or conduct any business activities within the country.[^cite_note-579]

### United Kingdom

On 12 July 2023, the [United Kingdom](31717.md) announced sanctions on firms linked to the SAF and the RSF.[^cite_note-2mf24-580]

### Canada

On 15 April 2024, [Canada](5042916.md) imposed sanctions on two individuals and four entities linked to the SAF and the RSF.[^cite_note-581] On 6 March 2025, Canada imposed sanctions on al-Burhan and Hemedti, due to "an unwillingness on the part of the leaders to negotiate an end to the war".[^cite_note-582]

### European Union

On 23 June 2024, the [European Union](9317.md) (EU) imposed sanctions on six entities for manufacturing and procuring weapons for the SAF and the RSF.[^cite_note-583] On 18 July 2025, the [European Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Council) (EUCO) adopted a fourth package of restrictive measures against two individuals and two entities, Alkhaleej Bank and Red Rock Mining Company.[^cite_note-584]
